Weakly inhomogeneous solutions to a nonlinear Vlasov–Fokker–Planck equation in a domain
Sihyun Song

Abstract
. A nonlinear Vlasov–Fokker–Planck equation is considered in a bounded domain, with specular reflection imposed on the boundary. It is proven that if the initial datum is sufficiently close, in a weighted L ∞ L^{\infty} -space, to a radial and spatially homogeneous function that is regular enough, then a corresponding global-in-time weak solution exists, and this one decays with exponential rate to the global Maxwellian. The proof is split into two steps. In the first, the stability of the manifold of spatially homogeneous solutions to this equation is established. In the second part, the close-to-Maxwellian regime is considered. A careful regularization scheme is designed so that this mollified equation can be well-approximated by a linear system. Inspired by the recent preprint [Carrapatoso and Mischler, arxiv:2407.09031], for this linear system, L 2 L^{2} -hypocoercivity along with ultracontractivity properties are derived in order to change the space of functional decay to the L ∞ L^{\infty} -framework. The properties of the original model are then recovered by means of fixed point arguments and carefully passing to the limit of the regularization parameter.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
The model and problem

We consider a nonlinear Vlasov–Fokker–Planck equation with specular reflection imposed at the boundary:

(1.1)

\begin{cases}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f=\mathscr{F}[f]&\text{in }\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\mathscr{F}[f]:=\rho[f]\nabla_{v}\cdot\Big(\theta[f]\nabla_{v}f+(v-u[f])f)\Big),\\
f|_{t=0}=f_{0}&\text{on }\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\gamma_{-}f(t,x,v)=\gamma_{+}(t,x,\mathscr{V}_{x}v)&\text{on }[0,\infty)\times\Sigma_{-}.\end{cases}

In the above, the coefficients of the equation are the macroscopic quantities associated to the solution, defined by

u[f]=\begin{cases}j[f]/\rho[f]&\rho[f]>0,\\
0&\rho[f]=0,\end{cases}\quad\rho[f]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,\textnormal{d}v,\quad j[f]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}vf\,\textnormal{d}v,

along with

\theta[f]:=\frac{1}{d\rho[f]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v-u[f]|^{2}f\,\textnormal{d}v=\frac{1}{d\rho[f]}\left(E[f]-\frac{|j[f]|^{2}}{\rho[f]}\right),\quad E[f]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v|^{2}f\,\textnormal{d}v.

The quantities
\rho[f]
,
u[f]
, and
\theta[f]
are often respectively referred to as the local density, bulk velocity, and temperature of
f
.

The Vlasov–Fokker–Planck equation is a fundamental equation which arises in the study of plasma physics or granular media
[
22
,
34
]
. It can be formally derived from the Landau equation

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=Q(f,f),\\
&Q(f,f):=\partial_{v_{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a_{ij}(v-v^{\prime})(f(v^{\prime})\partial_{v_{j}}f-f\partial_{v_{j}^{\prime}}f(v^{\prime}))\,\textnormal{d}v^{\prime}\end{split}

by replacing
f(v^{\prime})
with the corresponding local Maxwellian which corresponds to
f
, and by imposing that the matrix
a_{ij}
corresponds to the Maxwellian potential. This simplification results in that the relaxation towards equilibrium of solutions to (
1.1
) are expressed through quadratic terms which give a good approximation for binary collisions. However, as pointed out by Saint-Raymond in
[
42
]
, there has been no “satisfactory” existence theory for the model (
1.1
), especially in the framework of weak solutions.

In some simpler cases where the collision frequency is modified, for instance if one considers

\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=\nu[f]\nabla_{v}\cdot\Big(\theta[f]\nabla_{v}+(v-u[f]f)\Big)

with
\nu[f]
obeying some better bounds compared to
\rho[f]
, then some satisfactory results have appeared in the recent years. For the simplest case of
\nu[f]\equiv 1
and for
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, a gradient flow-type approach was implemented in
[
8
]
to prove the existence of (constructive) weak solutions under the assumption of
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{\langle v\rangle^{6}}\cap L^{2}\cap L\log L(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Under the different set of assumptions
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{\langle v\rangle^{3}}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L\log L(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the existence of weak solutions was proven also in
[
13
]
, under the same setting of
\nu\equiv 1
and
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. These results were subsequently generalized, in various directions, by the author in a joint work with Choi
[
14
]
. There, we considered initial datum satisfying only the physically natural assumptions of
L^{1}_{\langle v\rangle^{2}}\cap L\log L(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, more general (but bounded) collision frequencies
\nu
, and a general bounded domain
\Omega
. However, in that work, only the cases of fixed inflow or partial absorption-reflection boundary conditions were considered; the cases of pure specular reflection or Maxwell boundary conditions were not encompassed.

At this moment, the existence theory for
\eqref{eq: main}_{1}
yet appears to be out of reach for just physically admissible initial datum. Nevertheless, this work aims to provide a step forward in the existence theory by considering the equation in the weakly inhomogeneous regime. Namely, in this work, we prove that if the initial datum is sufficiently close to some spatially homogeneous and radial function
g_{0}
, then there exists a global-in-time weak solution to (
1.1
) which relaxes exponentially fast to the equilibrium state. The explicit main results will be stated in the next section.

1.2.
Notations and main results

Throughout this paper, we always impose the following assumptions and notations. The domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is assumed to be bounded, and we assume in particular that we can write
\Omega
as

\Omega:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathfrak{d}(x)>0\},

where
\mathfrak{d}(x):=\textnormal{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
is the signed distance function. We then define

n_{x}:=-\frac{\nabla\mathfrak{d}(x)}{|\nabla\mathfrak{d}(x)|},

which coincides with the unit outwards normal vector to
\Omega
whenever
x\in\partial\Omega
. We assume throughout that
\mathfrak{d}\in W^{2,\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
and that there exists some
c_{\Omega}>0
for which
|\nabla_{x}\mathfrak{d}|\geq c_{\Omega}
on all of
\overline{\Omega}
. The Hausdorff measure on
\partial\Omega
will be denoted
\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}
.

We remind the reader that the specular reflection boundary condition means that once particles touch the wall, they bounce away with an angle to the normal that is the same as with the angle of entry. Namely, the specular reflection boundary condition says that

(1.2)

\gamma_{-}f(t,x,v)=\gamma_{+}f(t,x,v-2(n_{x}\cdot v)n_{x})\quad\forall(t,x,v)\in(0,\infty)\times\Sigma_{-},

and we used the shorthand notation
\mathscr{V}_{x}v:=v-2(n_{x}\cdot v)n_{x}
in
\eqref{eq: main}_{2}
. The following notation is always adopted: for each
x\in\partial\Omega
,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\pm}^{x}:=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\pm v\cdot n_{x}>0\},

along with

\displaystyle\Sigma:=\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\Sigma_{\pm}:=\{(x,v)\in\Sigma:v\in\Sigma_{\pm}^{x}\},

\displaystyle\Sigma^{T}:=(0,T)\times\Sigma,\quad\Sigma^{T}_{\pm}:=(0,T)\times\Sigma_{\pm},\quad T\in(0,\infty).

We will also write
\mathcal{O}:=\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathcal{O}^{T}:=(0,T)\times\mathcal{O}
,
\Omega^{T}:=(0,T)\times\Omega
for given
T\in(0,\infty)
. Finally we remark that we often abuse notation and write (
1.2
) as

\gamma_{-}f=\gamma_{+}f\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}\quad\text{on }(0,\infty)\times\Sigma_{-}.

Regarding functional spaces, for a weight function
\omega
and
p\in[1,\infty]
we will use the notation

\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}}:=\|f\omega\|_{L^{p}}.

For Sobolev spaces we define
W^{1,p}_{\omega}
as those (equivalence class of) measurable functions for which the norm

\|f\|_{W^{1,p}_{\omega}}:=\Big(\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}}^{p}+\|\nabla f\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

is finite. Generalization to
W^{k,p}_{\omega}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, is given in the straightforward way. The domain or variable of integration is typically clear from the later context and we will not specify it at this point. In the case
p=2
we use the standard notation
H^{k}_{\omega}:=W^{k,2}_{\omega}
. Also, in integrals we will sometimes omit the differential form notation in order to save space if the context is clear, for instance

\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v=\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f.

For a Banach space
X
and a sequence
(f_{k})\subset X
, we will write
f_{k}\rightharpoonup f
weakly, or
f_{k}\rightharpoonup f
in
X-w
, if
f_{k}\to f
in
\sigma(X,X^{*})
. Similarly, for a sequence of functionals
(f_{k})\subset X^{*}
, we will write
f_{k}\rightharpoonup f
weakly
∗
, or
f_{k}\rightharpoonup f
in
X^{*}-*
, if
f_{k}\to f
in
\sigma_{*}(X^{*},X)
.

In regard of weak solutions, we will say that a function
f
is a weak solution to (
1.1
) if it satisfies
\eqref{eq: main}_{1}
in duality with
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}([0,t]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
for every
t\geq 0
with trace functions
f_{t}
and
\gamma f
, the latter of which satisfies the specular reflection boundary condition
\eqref{eq: main}_{2}
pointwisely (at least a.e.).

We also recall that the equation (
1.1
) formally satisfies certain conservation laws. The beginning observation is that

(1.3)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1,v,|v|^{2})\mathscr{F}[f]\,\textnormal{d}v=0

holds for any
f
which has sufficient regularity and decay. Consequently, if
f
is a smooth and rapidly decaying solution to (
1.1
) we immediately have the mass and energy conservation

(1.4)

\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\iint_{\mathcal{O}}(1,|v|^{2})f\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v=0,

thanks to the Gauss–Ostrogradsky theorem and the specular reflection boundary condition (
1.2
). In the case where
\Omega
has rotational symmetries, then another conservation law arises; namely, one can directly check that for
R(x):=Ux+u
, with
U\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the solution to (
1.1
) satisfies

(1.5)

\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\iint fR(x)\cdot v\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v=0

if and only if

(1.6)

\text{$U$ is skew symmetric and }R(x)\cdot n_{x}=0\quad\forall x\in\partial\Omega.

The proof of the above makes crucial use of the fact that
\int v\mathscr{F}[f]\,\textnormal{d}v=0
. We denote by
\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}
the collection of all such
R(x)=Ux+u
which satisfy (
1.6
), and we point out that
\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}
does not reduce to
\{0\}
in the case where rotational symmetries are present. In conclusion, the conservation laws of (
1.1
) can be summarized as

\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\iint_{\mathcal{O}}(1,R(x)\cdot v,|v|^{2})f\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v=0\quad\forall R\in\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}.

In view of the above, we impose the following condition on the initial datum. Denoting
\langle\!\langle f\rangle\!\rangle:=\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f
, we will say that
f
satisfies the compatibility condition (C) if the following holds:

\textnormal{(C)}\qquad\langle\!\langle f_{0}-\mu\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle(R(x)\cdot v)(f_{0}-\mu)\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle|v|^{2}(f_{0}-\mu)\rangle\!\rangle=0\quad\forall R\in\mathcal{R}_{\Omega},

where
\mu
is the usual normalized equilibrium
\mu(v):=e^{-|v|^{2}/2}/(2\pi)^{d/2}
.

Our main result reads as follows.

Theorem I
.

Let some radial function
g_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be given with
\|g_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{\langle v\rangle^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<+\infty
and
\rho[g_{0}]=1
. Then there exists a class of admissible weights
\mathcal{W}
such that the following holds: for
\omega\in\mathcal{W}
, if

\|g_{0}\|_{W^{2,\infty}_{\langle v\rangle\omega}}<+\infty,

then there exists
\delta>0
small enough such that if an initial datum
f_{0}
obeying the compatibility condition (C) satisfies

\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}\leq\delta,

there exists a global-in-time weak solution
f\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{O})
to (
1.1
) which satisfies (C) for all times
t\geq 0
. Furthermore, the following dissipative estimate holds

\forall t\geq 0,\quad\|f_{t}-\mu\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}\lesssim e^{-\iota^{\prime}t}

for some constant
\iota^{\prime}>0
independent of time.

Remark 1.1
.

The admissible class of weights
\mathcal{W}
in Theorem
I
is defined as

\mathcal{W}:=\mathcal{W}_{g_{0}}\cap\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}},

where for each
\mathfrak{g}:\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\|\mathfrak{g}\|_{L^{1}_{\langle v\rangle^{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty
, we define
\mathcal{W}_{\mathfrak{g}}
as

\mathcal{W}_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\left\{\begin{aligned} \langle v\rangle^{m},\quad&m>d+2,\\
\langle v\rangle^{m}e^{\gamma|v|^{\tau}},\quad&\text{either }m\geq 0,\;\gamma>0,\text{ and }\tau\in(0,2)\\
&\text{or }m\geq 0,\;\gamma\in\left(0,\frac{d}{2E[\mathfrak{g}]}\right),\text{ and }\tau=2\end{aligned}\right\}.

Remark 1.2
.

The uniqueness of the solution as constructed in Theorem
I
is unknown. If we impose some Sobolev regularity on the initial datum, then we believe that uniqueness can be established, but we do not pursue this direction in the current work.

The proof of Theorem
I
is split into two parts. In the first part, we consider the weakly-inhomogeneous regime in a finite time horizon setting. Namely, the main theorem of the first section establishes the stability of the manifold of spatially homogeneous solutions up to an arbitrary time:

Theorem II
.

We fix an admissible weight
\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{g_{0}}
. Let any
a\ll 1
,
T>0
be given, and assume that
g_{0}\in W^{2,\infty}_{\langle v\rangle\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then there exists
\delta=\delta(a,T)
such that if
\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq\delta(a,T)
, then there exists a weak and renormalized solution
f\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathcal{O})
to the Vlasov–Fokker–Planck system (
1.1
), satisfying

(1.7)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f_{t}-g_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}\leq a.

If, furthermore
f_{0}
satisfies the compatibility assumption (C), then
f_{t}
satisfies (C) also at every
t\in[0,T]
.

In the second part, we consider the close-to-Maxwellian regime. That is, we consider the setting where the initial datum is sufficiently close to
\mu
. We prove that a global-in-time weak solution exists and that it decays exponentially to the Maxwellian.

Theorem III
.

We fix an admissible weight
\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
. There exists some
a_{0}>0
small enough such that if
f_{0}
satisfies (C) and
\|f_{0}-\mu\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq a_{0}
, then there exists a weak and renormalized solution
f\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{O})
to the Vlasov–Fokker–Planck system (
1.1
). This one satisfies all of the conservation laws (C) and also the following hypodissipative estimate for some
\iota^{\prime}>0
:

(1.8)

\|f_{t}-\mu\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}\lesssim e^{-\iota^{\prime}t}.

The proof of Theorem
I
is then a simple combination of Theorems
II
–
III
.

Proof of Theorem
I
.
Since
\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{g_{0}}\cap\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
, we observe that both the results of Theorems
II
–
III
are at our disposal. Let
c_{\star}>0
be fixed as required in Theorem
III
. Thanks to the already known results on the spatially homogeneous Fokker–Planck equation (see Lemma
2.6
), we may also fix appropriate
T>0
so that

\displaystyle\|g_{T}-\mu\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq a_{0}/2.

Next, we apply Theorem
II
to find
\delta=\delta(a_{0},T)>0
such that if

\displaystyle\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq\delta,

then there exists a weak solution
f_{t}\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}([0,T]\times\mathcal{O})
to (
1.1
) satisfying

\|f_{T}-g_{T}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq a_{0}/2.

Then the triangle inequality shows

\displaystyle\|f_{T}-\mu\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq a_{0},

and thus we may now apply Theorem
III
(applied with initial datum
f_{T}
) to obtain a solution
f
which extends to
t\geq T
and exponentially converges to
\mu
as
t\to\infty
.
∎

1.3.
Strategy of proof and review of the literature

1.3.1.
A brief review

The Cauchy problem for kinetic equations and the long-time behavior of solutions is an important topic of research, and it has been investigated thoroughly in the past few decades. In regard of the model (
1.1
), results are relatively scarce but we may first mention
[
15
]
where a similar (but simpler) equation is considered, in the framework of high-order Sobolev spaces. Also, there is the more recent work
[
36
]
on a similar nonlinear Fokker–Planck equation to (
1.1
) in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and its trend to equilibrium is established. As aforementioned,
[
8
,
13
,
14
]
discussed the existence of weak solutions to a model similar to that of (
1.1
), but no rigorous results on the convergence
f\to\mu
were obtained.

Regarding more general models, the convergence (and its rate) of a solution to kinetic PDE to the equilibrium state has been extensively discussed, and this topic is typically called
hypocoercivity
; we refer to
[
19
,
20
,
23
,
39
]
and in particular the memoir
[
44
]
. In special regard of the Landau and Boltzmann equations, we may mention
[
9
,
16
,
26
,
33
,
43
]
; in regard of existence and trend to equilibrium problems posed on general domains, we can mention
[
4
,
7
,
10
,
11
,
27
,
24
,
32
,
31
]
.

There are various well-known methods which allow one to establish the hypocoercivity property of solutions. For instance we may mention the celebrated relative entropy method, in the spirit of Bakry–Emery. We refer to
[
30
]
for an introduction to entropy methods and
[
18
]
as another reference where entropy and
L^{2}
-estimates are appropriately interpolated. In the relative entropy framework, the goal is to find a suitable Lyapunov functional, equivalent to the usual entropy, which decays along the flow. This is actually not so simple as in the spatially homogeneous case because typically the dissipation of the entropy only provides relaxation towards a local equilibrium.

We also mention the
L^{2}
-hypocoercivity method, which is reminiscent of the procedure taken when investigating diffusion limits; there is a huge amount of references, and we just mention
[
1
,
4
,
20
]
at this point. It is worth pointing out that the recent work
[
4
]
was able to establish
L^{2}
-hypocoercivity in a general bounded domain, with Maxwell reflection condition imposed at the boundary, and this has been a source of enlightenment for
[
10
,
11
]
, and this work as well. There is also the
H^{1}
-hypocoercivity method, which incorporates a “twisted Fisher information,” popularized by Villani
[
44
]
and that of which is in the spirit of Hörmander’s work on hypoellipticity
[
28
]
.

Another interesting task in the theory of kinetic equations has been to obtain this hypocoercivity property in larger spaces, for instance spaces that are not Hilbert spaces. There are several motivations as to why such a topic is required. For one, while there have been groundbreaking works by Guo in high-order Sobolev spaces (see for instance
[
26
]
), these Sobolev spaces are not very well suited to boundary problems where we expect regularity loss to occur near grazing sets. Also, it is typically the case that the PDE in question is well-posed in weighted Lebesgue spaces (without having to extend to Sobolev regularity), and therefore these appear to be the natural spaces in which to derive decay of the solution. Consequently, many techniques have been developed in order to change the functional space in which decay holds, in particular
[
23
,
33
,
39
]
. It is worth emphasizing that a crucial ingredient in the proof of for instance
[
39
]
is the ultracontractivity property of kinetic equations. We remark that such an ultracontractivity property is well-known in the parabolic setting, thanks to the classical works of De Giorgi–Nash–Moser. Recently, the applications of their methods to the kinetic framework has seen light
[
21
]
. We refer in particular to the recent works
[
29
,
45
]
where regularity of some kinetic Fokker–Planck type equations is investigated.

The next two sections briefly describe the story behind the main results (Theorems
II
–
III
, respectively) and their proofs.

1.3.2.
The weakly inhomogeneous regime

The material of this section is inspired by
[
3
]
, where the Boltzmann equation was considered in such a weakly inhomogeneous regime; this was next generalized in
[
25
]
to bounded domains with specular reflection imposed on the boundary. In this regime, the main problem becomes a question of whether we can “keep small” perturbations about a general spatially inhomogeneous solution
g
to (
1.1
). In the case of the Fokker–Planck equation, a major difficulty is due to the fact that if one investigates the equation satisfied by the perturbation
h:=f-g
, then the principal part of the operator also becomes perturbed. In particular, the diffusion coefficient ends up with a quadratic term in
h
, which we denote
{\bf A}[h]
. If we are to work in a high-order Sobolev space as was done for instance in
[
26
]
, then we could just merely treat this term as a small nonlinear perturbation. However, our goal is to work only with weighted Lebesgue spaces, and so without any Sobolev space assumptions on the initial datum; thus, such an approach is not possible.

This difficulty is overcome through several steps. In
[
11
]
(where the Landau equation was discussed), the coefficients of the PDE were suitably modified so as the analysis reduces to that of a linear one. It turns out that trying to directly apply that approach does not work so well for the model (
1.1
). Indeed, as aforementioned, there is a quadratic term in the diffusion coefficient which prevents the application of any known fixed point theorems. Towards this end, instead of immediately linearizing this equation, we first mollify the quadratic diffusion coefficient
{\bf A}[h]
carefully, so that it now reads
{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h]
with a regularization parameter
\varsigma>0
. The method of mollification is specifically designed so that
{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[\cdot]
enjoys a strong stability property in suitable Lebesgue spaces, even if its argument only converges for instance weakly
-*
. This specific choice of mollification ensures that a fixed point argument becomes feasible, and we can then turn our attention solely to the linear counterpart of this mollified equation. That is, in the quadratic terms of the nonlinear PDE, we replace one in each term so that it depends on a fixed function
k=k(t,x,v)
instead of the solution
h
, and consequently to ensure that the resulting PDE becomes linear.

Thanks to preliminary results on linear nonlocal Kolmogorov-type equations, which we write out in Section
2
, we readily obtain the well-posedness for the linear equation in a weighted
L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}
framework, where
\widetilde{\omega}
is extracted from a set
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
which consists of polynomial weights. By manipulating the weights in
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
, in particular choosing two weights
\omega,\widetilde{\omega}
suitably so that
\widetilde{\omega}\omega^{-1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we extend the
L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}
solution to the
L^{\infty}_{\omega}
framework and rigorously establish
L^{\infty}_{\omega}
-estimates for it by resorting to its backwards dual equation and using a duality formula (Lemma
2.3
). This estimate can be made independent of
\varsigma
, and we prove that if the initial datum
h_{0}:=f_{0}-g_{0}
is small enough, then the solution operator leaves a small ball of
L^{\infty}_{\omega}([0,T]\times\mathcal{O})
invariant. Consequently, resorting to a fixed point theorem of Schauder, we obtain a solution to the nonlinear equation, which yet has mollified coefficient
{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h]
. We then pass to the limit
\varsigma\to 0
to show that a limit point exists which solves the original equation and satisfies the required stability estimate in (
1.7
).

1.3.3.
The close-to-Maxwellian regime

The second part of this work is inspired by the strategy that was taken in
[
11
]
regarding the Landau equation, see also
[
10
]
. In Section
4.2
we will provide a more explicit sketch of proof of Theorem
III
, and collect some of the key ingredients that are to be established later in the paper. Here, let us provide the reader with a rough draft of the scheme we will be using.

Similarly as in the weakly inhomogeneous regime, we write
h:=f-\mu
and consider the (nonlinear) PDE satisfied by
h
. Then, again similarly as before, we mollify the equation suitably so that some strong compactness can be expected in the coefficients of the PDE; as a result, a fixed point argument becomes feasible, and we can therefore turn our attention to the linear counterpart of this equation. Replacing the nonlinear coefficients appropriately, the analysis then reduces to that of a linear evolution equation

\partial_{t}h=\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]h,

where
k=k(t,x,v)
is assumed to be given and
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
is a suitable (time-dependent) operator, see Section
4.1
.

Then motivated by the so-called factorization of semigroups approach in
[
23
]
, we consider the decomposition
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]:=\mathsf{G}_{\varsigma}[k]+\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
where
\mathsf{G}_{\varsigma}[k]
is the regularizing part and
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
is the dissipative part of the operator. In Section
4.3
, we prove that indeed the evolution equation corresponding to
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
is well-posed in suitable weighted
L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}
spaces and that it is dissipative in all of those spaces, provided that
k
is small in the
L^{\infty}_{\omega}
-norm and the operator
\mathsf{G}_{\varsigma}[k]
is chosen appropriately. See Proposition
4.4
.

Now regarding the full operator
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
, we show in Sections
4.5
–
4.6
that it is well-posed in the reference space
L^{2}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
and that it enjoys the property of hypocoercivity in this space. However, in order to show that the dissipative property of
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
in
L^{2}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
extends to that of
L^{\infty}_{\omega}
, we aim to argue in the spirit of
[
39
]
and we therefore need to show that
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
enjoys an ultracontractive property. This is done in Section
4.4
, where we incorporate largely the arguments of those in
[
10
]
in order to make possible estimates in the spirit of De Giorgi–Nash–Moser. Thanks to the fact that we only consider the case of specular reflection boundary here, we are able to simplify some of the arguments there and prove an
L^{2}_{\mu^{-1/2}}\to L^{\infty}_{\omega}
-type estimate for the evolution system corresponding to
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
, which is the cornerstone step in extending the functional space of decay. We mention that such an argument is also reminiscent of the framework which has been developed in
[
24
,
33
]
.

As aforementioned, in Section
4.6
we establish the hypocoercivity property of
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
in
L^{2}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
, but this is not merely possible by putting together already known results. Indeed, although the “linearized operator” corresponding to
\mathscr{F}
is well-behaved and falls into the analysis of
[
4
]
, it turns out that
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
does not, and we need some more refined analysis in order to obtain a satisfactory coercivity estimate. For this reason, instead of resorting to known results, we go back to some elementary estimates related to the linear Fokker–Planck operator to recover some better energy estimates, see Lemma
4.14
. At last, using these refined estimates, we incorporate the framework developed in
[
4
]
in order to prove the existence of a twisted scalar product, equivalent to that of the original one on
L^{2}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
, for which the Dirichlet form associated to
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
is strictly coercive, see Proposition
4.12
.

Finally, in Section
4.7
we combine the results of Sections
4.4
–
4.5
–
4.6
, and use an extension trick in the spirit of
[
23
]
in order to prove that the evolution system associated to
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
extends to the
L^{\infty}_{\omega}
framework and that it is dissipative in this space. The remaining steps are then the same as with the weakly inhomogeneous regime: we perform a fixed point argument to recover a solution to the nonlinear (but mollified) problem, and then we pass the regularization parameter
\varsigma\to 0
in order to obtain a solution to the original problem. The properties of the solution
f
then follow by carefully passing everything that we established for the linear system to the nonlinear one.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Well-posedness of linear nonlocal Kolmogorov equations

We provide a general well-posedness result for Kolmogorov-type equations, in which the right-hand side depends also on the macroscopic moments of the solution. Similar results have already been mentioned in the recent preprint
[
11
]
, see also
[
12
]
. We present the material here in order to keep the presentation rather self-contained.

We shall consider a general linear operator of the form below, where differentiation acts only in the velocity variable:

\mathscr{L}f:=\sigma_{ij}\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}f+\zeta_{i}\partial_{v_{i}}f+\eta f,

along with a general nonlocal-in-
v
linear operator
\mathscr{P}
. For a typical example one can think of
\mathscr{P}f:=\rho[f]\mu
. Also, for convenience, we make use of the notation (for
p\in[1,\infty]
)

(2.1)

\begin{split}\Gamma_{\mathscr{L},\omega,p}(v)&:=2\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sigma_{ij}\frac{\partial_{v_{i}}\omega}{\omega}\frac{\partial_{v_{j}}\omega}{\omega}+\left(\frac{2}{p}-1\right)\sigma_{ij}\frac{\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}\omega}{\omega}+\frac{2}{p}\partial_{v_{j}}\sigma_{ij}\frac{\partial_{v_{i}}\omega}{\omega}\\
&\quad-\zeta_{i}\frac{\partial_{v_{i}}\omega}{\omega}+\frac{1}{p}\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}\sigma_{ij}-\frac{1}{p}\partial_{v_{i}}\zeta_{i}+\eta.\end{split}

The motivation behind this defect quantity lies in that it holds, at least formally, that [Lemma 2.5, CM24]

(2.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathscr{L}f)|f|^{p-2}f\,\omega^{p}\,\textnormal{d}v=-\frac{4(p-1)}{p^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}\widetilde{f}\partial_{v_{j}}\widetilde{f}\,\textnormal{d}v+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Gamma_{\mathscr{L},\omega,p}|f|^{p}\,\omega^{p}\,\textnormal{d}v,

where
\widetilde{f}:=f|f|^{\frac{p}{2}-1}\omega^{\frac{p}{2}}
.

We then assume the following conditions: for the weight, we impose

(W1)

\omega\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};(0,\infty))
. Moreover,
|\langle v\rangle^{-1}\nabla_{v}\omega|\lesssim\omega
and
|\langle v\rangle^{-2}\nabla_{v}^{2}\omega|\lesssim\omega
.

(W2)

\omega
is radial,
i.e.
there exists
\omega_{r}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to(0,\infty)
such that
\omega(v)=\omega_{r}(|v|)
.

Regarding the coefficients of
\mathscr{L}
, we assume that

(L1)

The matrix
\sigma(t,x,v)
is symmetric and there exists
\underline{\sigma}>0
such that
\sigma_{ij}{\bf x}_{i}{\bf x}_{j}\geq\underline{\sigma}|{\bf x}|^{2}
for all
{\bf x}=({\bf x}_{1},\ldots,{\bf x}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(L2)

There exist
\lambda_{\mathscr{L}}>0
and
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
such that the weight
\omega
satisfies

\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\Gamma_{\mathscr{L},\omega,2}(v)\leq\lambda_{\mathscr{L}}-\Lambda(v).

(L3)

There also holds

\langle v\rangle^{-1}\Big(|\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}\sigma_{ij}|+|\partial_{v_{i}}\sigma_{ij}|+|\sigma_{ij}|+|\partial_{v_{i}}\zeta_{i}|+|\zeta_{i}|+|\eta|\Big)\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}^{T}).

The system in concern is then a nonlocal Kolmogorov equation complemented with the specular reflection boundary condition (
1.2
):

(2.3)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=\mathscr{L}f+\mathscr{P}f+S,\\
\gamma_{-}f(t,x,v)=\gamma_{+}f(t,x,\mathscr{V}_{x}v),\end{cases}

The solution class is denoted as
\mathcal{H}_{\omega,T}
, the Hilbert space consisting of the (equivalence class of) functions for which the following norm

\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\omega,T}}^{2}:=\|h\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2}+\|h\|_{H^{1,\dagger}_{\omega,T}}^{2},\quad\|h\|_{H^{1,\dagger}_{\omega,T}}^{2}:=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Big(\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}(\omega h)\partial_{v_{j}}(\omega h)+\Lambda\omega^{2}h^{2}\Big)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t

is finite.

For later convenience, we set the notation

\displaystyle\textnormal{d}\nu_{1}:=\omega^{2}|v\cdot n_{x}|\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v.

The nonlocal operator
\mathscr{P}
is assumed to satisfy

(P1)

\|\mathscr{P}\|_{\mathscr{B}(L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T}))}=:\lambda_{\mathscr{P}}<\infty
.

Finally, the source term will be assumed to satisfy

(S1)

S\in L^{2}_{t,x}H^{-1}_{v,{\rm loc}}
. Namely,
S=S_{1}+\nabla_{v}\cdot S_{2}
where
S_{1},S_{2}\in L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
. Also, there exists
\lambda_{S}>0
such that

\|S_{1}\omega\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2}+\|S_{2}\nabla_{v}\omega\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\underline{\sigma}}\|S_{2}\omega\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2}\leq\lambda_{S}.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{L}
,
\mathscr{P}
, and
S
satisfy the conditions above. Then for each prescribed initial datum
f_{0}\in L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})
, there exists a corresponding unique weak solution
f\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}))\cap\mathcal{H}_{\omega,T}
to (
2.3
). The solution satisfies the equation
\eqref{p: e: kolmogorov}_{1}
in duality with
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O}^{T})
, and furthermore it is a renormalized solution.

Remark 2.2
.

By a renormalized solution to
\eqref{p: e: kolmogorov}_{1}
, we mean that for any
t\in(0,T]
,
\beta\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, and
\chi\in\mathcal{D}([0,t]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
, the following Green’s formula holds

(2.4)

\begin{split}&\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\beta(f_{t})\chi(t)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v-\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\beta(f_{0})\chi(0)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v+\iiint_{\Sigma^{t}}\beta(\gamma f)\,\chi\,(n_{x}\cdot v)\,\textnormal{d}s\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\\
&\quad+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}\left[\beta(f)\Big(-\partial_{t}\chi-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\chi-\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}(\sigma_{ij}\chi)-\partial_{v_{i}}(\zeta_{i}\chi)\Big)+\beta^{\prime\prime}(f)\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}f\partial_{v_{j}}f\,\chi\right]\,\textnormal{d}s\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\\
&=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}\beta^{\prime}(f)(\eta f+\mathscr{P}f)\,\chi\,\textnormal{d}s\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}(S_{1}-S_{2}\cdot\nabla_{v})(\beta^{\prime}(f)\chi)\,\textnormal{d}s\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v.\end{split}

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.
As aforementioned, similar arguments are available in
[
11
]
; we provide all details for the sake of completeness.

(Step 1: The inflow problem.) First, we consider the problem with fixed inflow

(2.5)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=\mathscr{L}f+\mathscr{P}f+S,\\
\gamma_{-}f=k\quad\text{on }\Sigma_{-}^{T},\\
f|_{t=0}=f_{0},\end{cases}

where
k\in L^{2}
. Then we dampen the equation enough to obtain coercivity: we set
\lambda>\lambda_{\mathscr{L}}+\lambda_{\mathscr{P}}
and
f_{\lambda}:=fe^{-\lambda t}
, in order to instead solve for

(2.6)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}f_{\lambda}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{\lambda}=\mathscr{L}f_{\lambda}+\mathscr{P}f_{\lambda}-\lambda f_{\lambda}+S_{\lambda},\\
S_{\lambda}:=Se^{-\lambda t},\\
\gamma_{-}f_{\lambda}=k_{\lambda}:=ke^{-\lambda t}\quad\text{on }\Sigma_{-}^{T},\\
f_{\lambda}|_{t=0}=f_{\lambda 0}:=f_{0}e^{-\lambda t}.\end{cases}

Next, we define on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega,T}\times C_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{T}\cup\Sigma_{-}^{T})
the bilinear and real-valued form

{\bf B}(h,\chi):=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Big(\lambda h\chi\omega^{2}-h\mathscr{L}^{*}(\chi\omega^{2})-\chi\omega^{2}\mathscr{P}h-h(\partial_{t}\chi+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\chi)\omega^{2}\Big).

In this way, to find a distributional solution to (
2.6
), it is enough to search for
f_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega,T}
which satisfies the variational problem

\begin{split}&\forall\chi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{O}^{T}\cup\Sigma_{-}^{T}),\\
&{\bf B}(f_{\lambda},\chi)=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}S_{\lambda}\chi\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t+\iiint_{\Sigma_{-}^{T}}k_{\lambda}\chi\omega^{2}(n(x)\cdot v)_{-}\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t+\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f_{\lambda 0}\chi(0)\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v.\end{split}

Thanks to the conditions (L2)–(P1)–(S1), we note that
{\bf B}
is coercive, since indeed

\displaystyle{\bf B}(\chi,\chi)

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Big(\lambda\chi^{2}\omega^{2}-\chi\mathscr{L}^{*}(\chi\omega^{2})-\chi\omega^{2}\mathscr{P}\chi\Big)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}(\lambda-\Gamma_{\mathscr{L},\omega,p}-\lambda_{\mathscr{P}})\chi^{2}\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathscr{L}}-\lambda_{\mathscr{P}})\chi^{2}\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t.

Then owing to Lions’ version of the Lax–Milgram theorem
[
37
, Chap III, §1]
, we deduce the existence of a solution
f_{\lambda}
to (
2.6
). Consequently
f:=f_{\lambda}e^{\lambda t}
is a distributional solution to (
2.5
). By virtue of (straightforward adaptations of) trace results in
[
41
]
, we then obtain that
f\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}))
and that
f
satisfies the renormalized Green’s formula as in (
2.4
).

(Step 2: Energy estimates.) Taking
\beta(z)\nearrow|z|^{2}
and
\chi(v)\nearrow 1
, we obtain the energy estimate

(2.7)

\begin{split}&\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f_{t}^{2}\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v-\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f_{0}^{2}\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v+2\|f\|_{H^{1,\dagger}_{\omega,t}}^{2}+\|\gamma f\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma^{t},\textnormal{d}\nu_{1})}^{2}\\
&\leq 2(\lambda_{\mathscr{L}}+\lambda_{\mathscr{P}})\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}f^{2}\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s+2\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}Sf\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s.\end{split}

For the integral containing the source term, we can estimate

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}Sf\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}S_{1}f\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s-\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}S_{2}\cdot\nabla_{v}(f\omega^{2})\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}S_{1}f\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s-\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}\Big(S_{2}\omega\cdot\nabla_{v}(f\omega)+S_{2}f\omega\cdot\nabla_{v}\omega\Big)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\|S_{1}\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{t})}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{t})}+\|S_{2}\omega\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{t})}\|\nabla_{v}(f\omega)\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{t})}+\|S_{2}\nabla_{v}\omega\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{t})}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{t})}

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{t})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\underline{\sigma}\|\nabla_{v}(\omega h)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lambda_{S}

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{t})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|f\|_{H^{1,\dagger}_{\omega,t}}^{2}+\lambda_{S}.

We point out that the second term concerning
\|f\|_{H_{\omega,t}^{1,\dagger}}^{2}
can be absorbed into the left-hand side of (
2.7
). Consequently, we obtain with
\widetilde{\lambda}:=2(\lambda_{\mathscr{L}}+\lambda_{\mathscr{P}}+1)
that

(2.8)

\begin{split}&\|f_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\gamma_{-}f\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{-},\textnormal{d}\nu_{1})}^{2}+\|f\|_{H^{1,\dagger}_{\omega,s}}^{2}\right)e^{\widetilde{\lambda}(t-s)}\,\textnormal{d}s\\
&\leq\|f_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}e^{\widetilde{\lambda}t}+\frac{2\lambda_{S}}{\widetilde{\lambda}}(e^{\widetilde{\lambda}t}-1)+\int_{0}^{t}\|k(s)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{-},\textnormal{d}\nu_{1})}e^{\widetilde{\lambda}(t-s)}\,\textnormal{d}s,\end{split}

which also implies uniqueness of the solution to (
2.5
). Indeed, if
f_{1},f_{2}
are two solutions to (
2.5
), then
f:=f_{2}-f_{1}
would solve (
2.5
) with inflow datum
k\equiv 0
, initial datum
f_{0}\equiv 0
, and source
S\equiv 0
. Thus we could apply (
2.8
) to conclude (noting that we can set
\lambda_{S}=0
).

(Step 3: Approximating the specular reflection boundary condition.) We let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathcal{H}_{\omega,T}\cap C([0,T];L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}))
be given, with trace
\gamma k\in L^{2}(\Gamma,\textnormal{d}\nu_{1})
. Then we consider the partial absorption-reflection problem

(2.9)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=\mathscr{L}f+\mathscr{P}f+S,\\
\gamma_{-}f(t,x,v)=\alpha\gamma_{+}k(t,x,\mathscr{V}_{x}v),\\
f|_{t=0}=f_{0}.\end{cases}

Thanks to the results in Steps 1–2, we deduce the existence and uniqueness of
f
the solution to the above problem. Similarly as with the end of Step 2, let
k=k_{1},k_{2}
in
\eqref{p: e: partial}_{2}
, and denote by
f_{1},f_{2}
the respective solutions to (
2.9
). Then
\mathfrak{f}:=f_{2}-f_{1}
is a solution to (
2.9
) with source term
S\equiv 0
, initial datum
\mathfrak{f}_{0}\equiv 0
, and boundary condition

\gamma_{-}\mathfrak{f}(t,x,v)=\alpha\gamma_{+}(k_{2}-k_{1})(t,x,\mathscr{V}_{x}v).

By using the energy estimate in (
2.8
) (with zero initial datum and source term), we get

\|\mathfrak{f}_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\gamma_{-}\mathfrak{f}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{-},\textnormal{d}\nu_{1})}^{2}\,\textnormal{d}s\leq\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\|k_{2}-k_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{-},\textnormal{d}\nu_{1})}^{2}e^{\widetilde{\lambda}(t-s)}\,\textnormal{d}s,

and therefore the map
k\mapsto f
is Lipschitz, with Lipschitz constant
\alpha
, with respect to the norm

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{2}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|f_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\gamma f_{s}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{-},\textnormal{d}\nu_{1})}^{2}e^{\widetilde{\lambda}(t-s)}\,\textnormal{d}s\right).

Since
\alpha<1
, we apply the contraction mapping theorem to deduce the existence and uniqueness of a fixed point of this map. This one is the unique distributional and renormalized solution to

(2.10)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=\mathscr{L}f+\mathscr{P}f+S,\\
\gamma_{-}f(t,x,v)=\alpha\gamma_{+}f(t,x,\mathscr{V}_{x}v),\\
f|_{t=0}=f_{0}.\end{cases}

For this solution, the same energy estimate as in (
2.8
) provides

(2.11)

\begin{split}&\|f_{t}\|_{L_{\widetilde{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\Big((1-\alpha)\|\gamma_{-}f_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{-},\textnormal{d}\nu_{1})}^{2}+\|f\|_{H^{1,\dagger}_{\omega,t}}^{2}\Big)e^{\widetilde{\lambda}(t-s)}\,\textnormal{d}s\\
&\leq\|f_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}^{2}e^{\widetilde{\lambda}t}+\frac{2\lambda_{S}}{\widetilde{\lambda}}(e^{\widetilde{\lambda}t}-1).\end{split}

We now set
\alpha=\alpha_{k}\in(0,1)
in
\eqref{p: e: alpha}_{2}
, with
\alpha_{k}\nearrow 1
. Denote by
f^{k}
the solution to (
2.10
) corresponding to
\alpha_{k}
. Thanks to the energy estimate in (
2.11
), we observe that we may admit
\beta(z)=z^{2}
and
\chi(x,v)=(n_{x}\cdot v)\omega^{2}\left<v\right>^{-4}
as an admissible pair into the renormalized formulation satisfied by the
f^{k}
. Owing to (W2), we have
\chi(x,\mathscr{V}_{x}v)=-\chi(x,v)
, thus

(2.12)

\begin{split}&\iint_{\mathcal{O}}|f^{k}_{t}\omega|^{2}(n_{x}\cdot v)\langle v\rangle^{-4}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v-\iint_{\mathcal{O}}|f^{k}_{0}\omega|^{2}(n_{x}\cdot v)\langle v\rangle^{-4}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\\
&\quad+(1+\alpha_{k})\iiint_{\Sigma_{+}^{t}}|\gamma_{+}f^{k}|^{2}(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\omega^{2}\langle v\rangle^{-4}\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s\\
&=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}|f^{k}\omega|^{2}\Big(v\cdot D_{x}n_{x}\,v\,\langle v\rangle^{-4}+\omega^{-2}\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}(\sigma_{ij}\chi)+\omega^{-2}\partial_{v_{i}}(\zeta_{i}\chi)\Big)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s\\
&\quad+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}2\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}f^{k}\partial_{v_{j}}f^{k}\,\chi\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}2f^{k}(\eta f^{k}+\mathscr{P}f^{k})\chi\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s\\
&\quad+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}(S_{1}-S_{2}\cdot\nabla_{v})(2f^{k}\chi)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s.\end{split}

Using the energy bounds in (
2.11
), along with (W1)–(L3)–(P1)–(S1), we can readily check that every term involving
f^{k}
is bounded uniformly in
k
. For instance,

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}2\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}f^{k}\partial_{v_{j}}f^{k}|n(x)\cdot v|\omega^{2}\left<v\right>^{-4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|\nabla_{v}f^{k}|^{2}\omega^{2}\langle v\rangle^{-2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|\omega\nabla_{v}f^{k}|^{2}\langle v\rangle^{-2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\left|\nabla_{v}(f^{k}\omega)-f^{k}\nabla_{v}\omega\right|^{2}\langle v\rangle^{-2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Big(|\nabla_{v}(f^{k}\omega)|^{2}+(f^{k})^{2}\left|\langle v\rangle^{-1}\nabla_{v}\omega\right|^{2}\Big)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Big(|\nabla_{v}(f^{k}\omega)|^{2}+|f^{k}|^{2}\omega^{2}\Big)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C.

From (
2.12
) we can therefore deduce

\int_{\Sigma_{+}^{T}}|\gamma_{+}f^{k}|^{2}(n(x)\cdot v)^{2}\omega^{2}\left<v\right>^{-4}\,\textnormal{d}t\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\leq C.

Since
\gamma_{-}f^{k}=\alpha_{k}(\gamma_{+}f^{k}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x})
, this also implies

\int_{\Sigma_{-}^{T}}|\gamma_{-}f^{k}|^{2}(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\omega^{2}\langle v\rangle^{-4}\,\textnormal{d}t\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\leq C\alpha_{k}^{2}\leq C.

Therefore, there are limits
\xi_{\pm}
such that modulo a subsequence

(2.13)

\gamma_{\pm}f^{k}\rightharpoonup\xi_{\pm}\quad\text{in}\quad L^{2}(\Sigma^{T},(n(x)\cdot v)^{2}\omega^{2}\left<v\right>^{-4}\textnormal{d}t\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v)-w.

To see that
\xi_{-}=\xi_{+}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}
, we write

\displaystyle\xi_{-}-\xi_{+}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}

\displaystyle=(\xi_{-}-\gamma_{-}f^{k})+(\gamma_{-}f^{k}-\gamma_{+}f^{k}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x})+(\gamma_{+}f^{k}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}-\xi_{+}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x})

\displaystyle=(\xi_{-}-\gamma_{-}f^{k})+(\alpha_{k}-1)\gamma_{+}f^{k}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}+(\gamma_{+}f^{k}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}-\xi_{+}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}).

Then, using the weak convergences in (
2.13
) and the fact that
\alpha_{k}\nearrow 1
, we can prove that
\xi_{-}=\xi_{+}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}
in the sense of distributions and thus a.e.

Now passing to the limit
\alpha_{k}\nearrow 1
in all equations of (
2.10
) (with the help of (P1)), we obtain that the limit
f
satisfies the following Green’s formula in duality with
\chi\in\mathcal{D}((0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
:

(2.14)

\begin{split}&\iiint_{\Sigma^{T}}\xi\chi(n_{x}\cdot v)\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t\\
&=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Big(f(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\mathscr{L}^{*})\chi+(\mathscr{P}f+S)\chi\Big)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t.\end{split}

In particular
f
is a solution to
\eqref{p: e: kolmogorov}_{1}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
. Owing to the facts that
f\in L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v,{\rm loc}}
and
S\in L^{2}_{t,x}H^{-1}_{v,{\rm loc}}
, we may straightforwardly adapt
[
41
, Theorem 4.2]
once again, to deduce that
f
is in fact a renormalized solution with unique traces
f_{t}
and
\gamma f
which satisfy (
2.4
) for every
\chi\in\mathcal{D}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
. Choosing
\chi\in\mathcal{D}((0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
, we in particular obtain from (
2.4
) that

\displaystyle\iiint_{\Sigma^{T}}\beta(\gamma f)\chi(n_{x}\cdot v)\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\left[\beta(f)\Big(\partial_{t}\chi+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\chi+\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}(\sigma_{ij}\chi)+\partial_{v_{i}}(\zeta_{i}\chi)\Big)-\beta^{\prime\prime}(f)\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}f\partial_{v_{j}}f\,\chi\right]\,\textnormal{d}t\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v

\displaystyle\quad+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\beta^{\prime}(f)(\eta f+\mathscr{P}f)\,\chi\,\textnormal{d}t\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{t}}(S_{1}-S_{2}\cdot\nabla_{v})(\beta^{\prime}(f)\chi)\,\textnormal{d}t\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v,

for every
\beta\in C^{2}\cap W^{2,\infty}
. Since
f
is already a weak solution, and since
\gamma f\in L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma^{T},|n_{x}\cdot v|\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t)
, we can choose an approximating sequence
\beta(z)\nearrow z
in order to deduce that

(2.15)

\begin{split}&\iiint_{\Sigma^{T}}\gamma f\,\chi\,(n_{x}\cdot v)\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t\\
&\quad+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Big(f(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\mathscr{L}^{*})\chi+(\mathscr{P}f+S)\chi\Big)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t.\end{split}

Comparing (
2.15
) with (
2.14
), we deduce that
\xi(n_{x}\cdot v)=\gamma f(n_{x}\cdot v)
in the sense of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
, and consequently a.e. in
\Sigma^{T}_{\pm}
. Recalling that
\xi_{-}=\xi_{+}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}
we immediately deduce that
\gamma_{-}f=\gamma_{+}f\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}
also. In particular, we have proven that
f
is a weak and renormalized solution to

(2.16)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=\mathscr{L}f+\mathscr{P}f+S,\\
\gamma_{-}f=\gamma_{+}f\circ\mathscr{V}_{x},\\
f|_{t=0}=f_{in}.\end{cases}

As before we obtain also that
f\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}))
by the arguments in
[
41
]
.

(Step 4: Uniqueness.)
We consider two solutions to the system (
2.16
) with the same initial datum
f_{0}
; then their difference, which we denote
\mathfrak{f}
, is again a solution to (
2.16
), now with both initial datum and source term zero. Then, we can obtain an energy estimate for
\mathfrak{f}
that is similar to that of (
2.11
), but with
\alpha=1
,
\mathfrak{f}_{0}\equiv 0
, and
\lambda_{S}\equiv 0
. We deduce uniqueness in the class
C([0,T];L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}))
, as desired.
∎

For later use, we also mention a useful fact on the following backwards adjoint equation, complemented with the dual specular reflection boundary condition:

(2.17)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}b-v\cdot\nabla_{x}b=\mathscr{L}^{*}b+\mathscr{P}^{*}b\quad\text{in }\mathcal{O}^{T},\\
\gamma_{+}b=\gamma_{-}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}\quad\text{on }\Sigma_{+}^{T}.\end{cases}

In the above, we denote by
\mathscr{P}^{*}
the formal adjoint operator of
\mathscr{P}
. We assume
\mathscr{P}^{*}\in\mathscr{B}(L^{2}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}^{T}))
and that

\iint_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathscr{P}f)b\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v=\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f\,\mathscr{P}^{*}b\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v

holds for any
f\in L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})
and
b\in L^{2}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathcal{O})
.

The result we mention is a duality formula.

Lemma 2.3
.

We assume that
f\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}))\cap L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is a solution to (
2.3
), corresponding to initial datum
f_{0}\in L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})
. Analogously, we suppose that
b\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}))\cap L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is a solution to the backwards equation (
2.17
) corresponding to terminal datum
b_{T}\in L^{2}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathcal{O})
. On the weight, let us assume

(W1’)

\omega\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};(0,\infty))
,
\omega\gtrsim 1
, and
|\nabla_{v}\omega|\lesssim\omega
.

Then under the assumptions (W1’)–(W2), (L1)–(L2)–(L3), and (P1), it holds that

\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f_{T}b_{T}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v=\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f_{0}b_{0}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}Sb\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t,

whenever the last integral of the right-hand side is finite.

Remark 2.4
.

In practice, we will usually consider a polynomial weight
\omega=\langle v\rangle^{m}
so that (W1’) is satisfied. Also, typically
m
will be taken large enough so that
L^{2}_{\langle v\rangle^{m}}\hookrightarrow L^{1}_{\langle v\rangle^{2}}
. This inclusion is important later on for the weight to be compatible with the nonlocal operator
\mathscr{P}
, in particular to ensure that the condition (P1) is satisfied.

Proof of Lemma
2.3
.
(Step 1: Regularizing the problem.)
Consider mollified versions
f_{\varepsilon}
and
b_{\varepsilon}
of
f
and
b
, respectively. Namely, let

(f)_{\varepsilon}=f_{\varepsilon}:=f\star_{\varepsilon}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}*_{v}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon},

where
({\bf r}_{\varepsilon})
is the usual family of mollifiers supported in the ball
B_{\varepsilon}
, and the convolution-translation operation
\star_{\varepsilon}
is defined in the spatial variable by (see for instance
[
5
]
)

\displaystyle\forall x\in\overline{\Omega},\quad f\star_{\varepsilon}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}(x):=\int_{\Omega}f(y){\bf r}_{\varepsilon}(x-2\varepsilon n_{x}-y)\,\textnormal{d}y.

For each fixed
(x,v)\in\mathcal{O}
, we observe that

{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}(x-2\varepsilon n_{x}-y){\bf r}_{\varepsilon}(v-w)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O}_{y,w}),

and thus, using this as test function in the distributional formulation of
f
, we obtain that
f_{\varepsilon}
is a solution to

(2.18)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}f_{\varepsilon}+(v\cdot\nabla_{x}f)_{\varepsilon}&=(f\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}\sigma_{ij})_{\varepsilon}-2(f\partial_{v_{j}}\sigma_{ij})\star_{\varepsilon}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}*_{v}\partial_{v_{i}}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}+(f\sigma_{ij})\star_{\varepsilon}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}*_{v}\partial_{v_{i}v_{j}}^{2}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}\\
&\quad+(f\nabla_{v}\cdot\zeta)\star_{\varepsilon}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}*_{v}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}-(f\zeta)\star_{\varepsilon}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}*_{v}\nabla_{v}\cdot{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}\\
&\quad+(\eta f)_{\varepsilon}+(\mathscr{P}f)_{\varepsilon}+(S)_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}}).\end{split}

From this very equation it follows that
f_{\varepsilon}\in W^{1,1}((0,T);W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}))
. We may therefore assume without loss of generality that
f_{\varepsilon}
is continuous on
[0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}}
, and thus
f_{\varepsilon}
admits traces
\gamma f_{\varepsilon}
and
f_{\varepsilon t}
. Next, we observe that
f_{\varepsilon}\to f
in
L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v,\rm loc}
by standard property of the mollifier. We claim that this implies
\gamma f_{\varepsilon}\to\gamma f
strongly in a suitable norm. Indeed, observe that the PDE for
f_{\varepsilon}
may be rewritten as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{\varepsilon}=\mathscr{L}f_{\varepsilon}+(\mathscr{P}f)_{\varepsilon}+(S)_{\varepsilon}+R_{\varepsilon}^{1},

where
R_{\varepsilon}^{1}
is the remainder term (commutator)

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}^{1}

\displaystyle:=v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{\varepsilon}-(v\cdot\nabla_{x}f)_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\quad-2\partial_{v_{i}}\Big((f\partial_{v_{j}}\sigma_{ij})_{\varepsilon}-f_{\varepsilon}\partial_{v_{j}}\sigma_{ij}\Big)+\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}\Big((f\sigma_{ij})_{\varepsilon}-f_{\varepsilon}\sigma_{ij}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\Big((f\nabla_{v}\cdot\zeta)_{\varepsilon}-f_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{v}\cdot\zeta\Big)+\nabla_{v}\cdot\Big((f\zeta)_{\varepsilon}-f_{\varepsilon}\zeta\Big)+\Big((\eta f)_{\varepsilon}-\eta f_{\varepsilon}\Big).

Thanks to the bounds of (L3), the arguments of
[
17
,
41
]
show that
R_{\varepsilon}^{1}\to 0
at least in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathcal{O}^{T})+L^{2}_{t,x}H^{-1}_{v,{\rm loc}}
. Now using this PDE formulation, and noting that the chain rule applies to
f_{\varepsilon}
(since
f_{\varepsilon}\in W^{1,1}_{t}W^{1,\infty}_{x,v,{\rm loc}}
), we may obtain estimates in the manner of (
2.12
). Namely, using the test function
\varphi_{R}(x,v):=(n_{x}\cdot v)\omega^{2}\chi_{R}(v)
where
\chi_{R}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{R+1})
and
\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq R}\leq\chi_{R}\leq\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq R+1}
, we obtain (using the notation
\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R}:=(0,T)\times\Omega\times B_{R}
)

(2.19)

\begin{split}&\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R}}|\gamma f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t\\
&\lesssim_{R,\omega}\iint_{\Omega\times B_{R+1}}(|f_{\varepsilon T}|^{2}+|f_{\varepsilon 0}|^{2})\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\\
&\quad+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}|f_{\varepsilon}\omega|^{2}\Big|v\cdot D_{x}n_{x}\,v+\omega^{-2}\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}(\sigma_{ij}\varphi_{R})+\omega^{-2}\partial_{v_{i}}(\zeta_{i}\varphi_{R})\Big|\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t\\
&\quad+2\left|\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}\Big(\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}f_{\varepsilon}\partial_{v_{j}}f_{\varepsilon}+\eta f_{\varepsilon}^{2}+2f_{\varepsilon}(\mathscr{P}f)_{\varepsilon}\Big)\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t\right|\\
&\quad+\left|\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}((S)_{\varepsilon}+R_{\varepsilon}^{1})(2f_{\varepsilon}\varphi_{R})\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t\right|.\end{split}

Using the bounds in (W1)–(L3)–(P1), and noting that the domain of integration is bounded, we can crudely bound the terms in the right-hand side of (
2.19
) in the following manner. First,

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}|f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\Big|v\cdot D_{x}n_{x}\,v+\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}(\sigma_{ij}\varphi_{R})+\partial_{v_{i}}(\zeta_{i}\varphi_{R})\Big|\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t\leq C_{R,\omega}\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}|f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t,

\displaystyle\left|\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}f_{\varepsilon}\partial_{v_{j}}f_{\varepsilon}\omega^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t\right|\leq C_{R,\omega}\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}|\nabla_{v}f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t.

Next,

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}|\eta f_{\varepsilon}^{2}+f_{\varepsilon}(\mathscr{P}f)_{\varepsilon}|\omega^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}^{2}+\left(\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}|f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\omega^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}|(\mathscr{P}f)_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\omega^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}^{2}+C_{R}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}\left(\iiint_{\mathcal{O}_{R+1}^{T}}|(\mathscr{P}f)_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}^{2}+C_{R}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}\left(\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1+\varepsilon}}|\mathscr{P}f|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}^{2}+C_{R,\omega}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}\left(\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1+\varepsilon}}|\mathscr{P}f|^{2}\omega^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}^{2}+\lambda_{\mathscr{P}}\,C_{R,\omega}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}.

In the above, the first inequality is just Cauchy–Schwarz; the second is because
1\lesssim\omega\lesssim 1
on
B_{R+1}
; the third inequality is a standard computation which follows from the definition of the mollifier; the fourth inequality uses again that
1\lesssim\omega
, and the last inequality owes to (P1). For the final term in (
2.19
), we have:

\displaystyle\left|\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1}}((S)_{\varepsilon}+R_{\varepsilon}^{1})(2f_{\varepsilon}\varphi_{R})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|S\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{-1}(B_{R+1}))}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}(B_{R+1}))}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{R}\|R_{\varepsilon}^{1}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{-1}(B_{R+1}))}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}(B_{R+1}))}.

by using Young’s convolution inequality. Consequently,

\displaystyle\iiint_{(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times B_{R}}|\gamma f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\omega^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{R}\|f_{\varepsilon T}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times B_{R+1})}^{2}+\|f_{\varepsilon 0}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times B_{R+1})}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}(B_{R+1}))}^{2}+\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}

\displaystyle\quad+\|S\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{-1}(B_{R+1}))}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}(B_{R+1}))}

\displaystyle\quad+\|R_{\varepsilon}^{1}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{-1}(B_{R+1}))}\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}(B_{R+1}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{R,T}\|f_{\varepsilon T}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times B_{R+1})}^{2}+\|f_{\varepsilon 0}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times B_{R+1})}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}(B_{R+1}))}^{2}+\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}

\displaystyle\quad+\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}(B_{R+1}))}+o(\varepsilon),

the last inequality due to the facts that
f\in L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})
,
S\in L^{2}_{t,x}H^{-1}_{v,{\rm loc}}
, and because
(f_{\varepsilon})
is bounded in
L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v,{\rm loc}}
. Now owing to the linear nature of the problem, for any
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}>0
we may apply this estimate to
f_{\varepsilon_{2}}-f_{\varepsilon_{1}}
. Then there holds

(2.20)

\begin{split}&\iiint_{(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times B_{R}}|\gamma f_{\varepsilon_{2}}-\gamma f_{\varepsilon_{1}}|^{2}(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\omega^{2}\\
&\lesssim\|f_{\varepsilon_{2}T}-f_{\varepsilon_{1}T}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times B_{R+1})}^{2}+\|f_{\varepsilon_{2}0}-f_{\varepsilon_{1}0}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times B_{R+1})}^{2}\\
&\quad+\|f_{\varepsilon_{2}}-f_{\varepsilon_{1}}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}(B_{R+1}))}^{2}+\|f_{\varepsilon_{2}}-f_{\varepsilon_{1}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T}_{R+1})}\\
&\quad+\|f_{\varepsilon_{2}}-f_{\varepsilon_{1}}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}(B_{R+1}))}+o(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}).\end{split}

Using that
(f_{\varepsilon t})
is Cauchy in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\overline{\mathcal{O}})
and
(f_{\varepsilon})
is Cauchy in
L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we deduce that
(\gamma f_{\varepsilon})
is Cauchy in
L^{2}((0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times B_{R},(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\omega^{2}\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t)
. Consequently there is a limit
\xi
for which, up to a diagonal extraction,
\gamma f_{\varepsilon}\to\xi
in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma^{T};(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t)
and a.e. We can then consider the Green’s formula satisfied by
f_{\varepsilon}
in duality with
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
, and pass to the limit, to deduce that
f
satisfies
\eqref{p: e: kolmogorov}_{1}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
with trace
\xi
. Such a trace is unique (an immediate consequence of the Green’s formula), and therefore we conclude to
\xi=\gamma f
.

Because all of the estimates above are local in nature, and since
b\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;H^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we can proceed with the dual backwards equation in the same manner. We first have that
b_{\varepsilon}
is a solution to

-\partial_{t}b_{\varepsilon}-v\cdot\nabla_{x}b_{\varepsilon}=\mathscr{L}^{*}b_{\varepsilon}+(\mathscr{P}^{*}b)_{\varepsilon}+R_{\varepsilon}^{2}

in the sense of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
. Here the commutator satisfies (at least) that
R_{\varepsilon}^{2}\to 0
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}+L^{2}_{t,x}H^{-1}_{v,{\rm loc}}
; we can also prove, similarly as we did for
f_{\varepsilon}
, that
\gamma b_{\varepsilon}\to\gamma b
strongly in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma^{T};(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t)
and a.e. as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
.

(Step 2: Renormalizing into an
L^{\infty}
-framework.) Now we may argue by adapting the framework of
[
17
]
and
[
40
]
. We take an odd renormalizing function

\beta_{M}(z):=\begin{cases}z&|z|\leq M,\\
M+1&|z|\geq M+2,\end{cases}

defined suitably on
(-(M+2),-M)\cup(M,M+2)
so that
\beta_{M}\in C^{2}\cap W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Then in the sense of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})+\beta_{M}^{\prime}(f_{\varepsilon})((\mathscr{P}f)_{\varepsilon}+(S)_{\varepsilon}+R_{\varepsilon}^{1})

\displaystyle\quad-\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(f_{\varepsilon})\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}f_{\varepsilon}\partial_{v_{j}}f_{\varepsilon}+\eta(\beta_{M}^{\prime}(f_{\varepsilon})f_{\varepsilon}-\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})),

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}^{*}\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})+\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})((\mathscr{P}^{*}b)_{\varepsilon}+R_{\varepsilon}^{2})

\displaystyle\quad+(b_{\varepsilon}\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})-\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon}))\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}\sigma_{ij}-\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}g\partial_{v_{j}}g

\displaystyle\quad+(b_{\varepsilon}\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})-\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon}))(\nabla_{v}\cdot\zeta)

\displaystyle\quad+\eta(b_{\varepsilon}\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})-\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})).

Furthermore, going back to the formulation in (
2.18
), it is clear that
\beta_{M}\in W^{2,\infty}
implies

\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon}),\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})\in W^{1,1}((0,T),W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}))\cap L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}}),

and therefore the product rule applies to
\mathfrak{P}_{M,\varepsilon}:=\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})
. We obtain namely that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathfrak{P}_{M,\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\mathfrak{P}_{M,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\partial_{v_{i}}\Big(\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{j}}\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})-\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})\partial_{v_{j}}(\sigma_{ij}\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon}))+\zeta_{i}\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})\beta_{M}^{\prime}(f_{\varepsilon})\Big((\mathscr{P}f)_{\varepsilon}+(S)_{\varepsilon}+R_{\varepsilon}^{1}\Big)+\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})\beta_{M}^{\prime}(f_{\varepsilon})\Big((\mathscr{P}^{*}b)_{\varepsilon}+R_{\varepsilon}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})\Big(-\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(f_{\varepsilon})\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}f_{\varepsilon}\partial_{v_{j}}f_{\varepsilon}+\eta(\beta_{M}^{\prime}(f_{\varepsilon})f_{\varepsilon}-\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon}))\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})\Big((b_{\varepsilon}\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})-\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon}))\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}\sigma_{ij}-\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}g\partial_{v_{j}}g\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})\Big((b_{\varepsilon}\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})-\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon}))(\nabla_{v}\cdot\zeta)+\eta(b_{\varepsilon}\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})-\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon}))\Big).

(Step 3: Passing to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
.) Writing out explicitly the Green’s formula satisfied by
\mathfrak{P}_{M,\varepsilon}
in duality with
\mathcal{D}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
, let us first aim to pass to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
. Observe that as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

(2.21)

\begin{split}&\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon t})\to\beta_{M}(f_{t})\quad\text{a.e. in $\mathcal{O}$ for all $t\in[0,T]$},\\
&\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})\to\beta_{M}(f),\quad\beta_{M}^{\prime}(f_{\varepsilon})\to\beta_{M}^{\prime}(f),\quad\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(f_{\varepsilon})\to\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(f)\quad\text{a.e. in }(0,T)\times\mathcal{O},\\
&\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})\to\beta_{M}(b),\quad\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})\to\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b),\quad\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(b_{\varepsilon})\to\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(b)\quad\text{a.e. in }(0,T)\times\mathcal{O},\\
&\beta_{M}(\gamma f_{\varepsilon})\to\beta_{M}(\gamma f),\quad\beta_{M}(\gamma b_{\varepsilon})\to\beta_{M}(\gamma b)\quad\text{a.e. in }\Sigma^{T},\end{split}

at least up to a subsequence. Now, all sequences in (
2.21
) are trivially uniformly-in-
\varepsilon
bounded in
L^{\infty}
(by definition of
\beta_{M}
). Therefore, by the convergence theorem of Vitali, the convergences in (
2.21
) are true in
L^{p}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
for any
p\in[1,\infty)
.

Also, since
\mathscr{P}f,\mathscr{P}^{*}b\in L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
and
S\in L^{2}_{t,x}H^{-1}_{v,{\rm loc}}
, it is clear that

\begin{split}&(\mathscr{P}f)_{\varepsilon}\to\mathscr{P}f\quad\text{in}\quad L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}}),\\
&(\mathscr{P}^{*}b)_{\varepsilon}\to\mathscr{P}^{*}b\quad\text{in}\quad L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}}),\\
&(S)_{\varepsilon}\to S\quad\text{in}\quad L^{2}([0,T]\times\Omega;H^{-1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).\end{split}

For the trace
\gamma\mathfrak{P}_{M,\varepsilon}
we may discuss as follows. Note that
f_{\varepsilon}
and
b_{\varepsilon}
may be assumed to be (uniformly) continuous in each compact subset of
\overline{\mathcal{O}}
(by Sobolev embedding). Therefore
\beta_{M}(f_{\varepsilon})
,
\beta_{M}(b_{\varepsilon})
are also continuous on
\overline{\mathcal{O}}
, which means
\gamma\mathfrak{P}_{M,\varepsilon}=\beta_{M}(\gamma f_{\varepsilon})\beta_{M}(\gamma b_{\varepsilon})
. It follows, again merely by definition of
\beta_{M}
, that
(\gamma\mathfrak{P}_{M,\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(\Sigma^{T})
. Combining this with
\eqref{P: E: beta M ve}_{4}
(which yields that
\gamma\mathfrak{P}_{M,\varepsilon}\to\beta_{M}(\gamma f)\beta_{M}(\gamma b)
a.e.), we obtain again through Vitali’s theorem that
\gamma\mathfrak{P}_{M,\varepsilon}\to\beta_{M}(\gamma f)\beta_{M}(\gamma b)
strongly in
L^{p}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma^{T})
for every
p\in[1,\infty)
.

Using all of these strong convergences, we can now easily pass to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
in every term of the Green’s formula that is satisfied by
\mathfrak{P}_{M,\varepsilon}
. We deduce that
\mathfrak{P}_{M}:=\beta_{M}(f)\beta_{M}(b)
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathfrak{P}_{M}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\mathfrak{P}_{M}

\displaystyle=\partial_{v_{i}}\Big(\beta_{M}(b)\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{j}}\beta_{M}(f)-\beta_{M}(f)\partial_{v_{j}}(\sigma_{ij}\beta_{M}(b))+\zeta_{i}\beta_{M}(f)\beta_{M}(b)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{M}(b)\beta_{M}^{\prime}(f)(\mathscr{P}f+S)-\beta_{M}(f)\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b)\mathscr{P}^{*}b

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{M}(b)\Big(-\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(f)\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}f\partial_{v_{j}}f+\eta(\beta_{M}^{\prime}(f)f-\beta_{M}(f))\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{M}(f)\Big((b_{\varepsilon}\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b)-\beta_{M}(b))\partial^{2}_{v_{i}v_{j}}\sigma_{ij}-\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(b)\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{i}}g\partial_{v_{j}}g\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{M}(f)\Big((b\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b)-\beta_{M}(b))(\nabla_{v}\cdot\zeta)+\eta(b\beta_{M}^{\prime}(b)-\beta_{M}(b))\Big)

in duality with
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
with unique trace functions
\mathfrak{P}_{Mt}=\beta_{M}(f_{t})\beta_{M}(b_{t})
and
\gamma\mathfrak{P}_{M}=\beta_{M}(\gamma f)\beta_{M}(\gamma b)
.

(Step 4: Passing to the limit
M\to\infty
and then
\varphi\nearrow 1
.) We now specify the test function
\varphi(x,v)=\chi_{R}(v)
, where
\chi_{R}
is a radial function satisfying

(2.22)

\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq R}\leq\chi_{R}\leq\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq 2R},\quad|\nabla_{v}\chi_{R}|\leq\frac{2}{R}\mathbf{1}_{R\leq|v|\leq 2R},\quad|\nabla_{v}^{2}\chi_{R}|\leq\frac{10}{R^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{R\leq|v|\leq 2R},

with
\chi_{R}\nearrow 1
as
R\to\infty
. Note that in particular, the radial symmetry of
\chi_{R}
and the dual reflection boundary conditions imply

-\iint_{\Sigma}\beta_{M}(\gamma f)\beta_{M}(\gamma b)\chi_{R}(v\cdot n_{x})\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v=0.

Thus in the Green’s formula satisfied by
\mathfrak{P}_{M}
and
\chi_{R}
, the boundary integral may be discarded. Then using the bounds

\displaystyle|\beta_{M}(z)|\leq|z|,\quad|\beta_{M}^{\prime}(z)|\lesssim 1,\quad|\beta_{M}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\lesssim 1,

and owing to the fact that
f,b\in L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v}
, we can apply the dominated convergence theorem of Lebesgue to pass to the limit
M\to\infty
in this Green’s formula, obtaining

(2.23)

\begin{split}&\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f_{T}b_{T}\chi_{R}-\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f_{0}b_{0}\chi_{R}\\
&=-\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Big(b\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{j}}f-f\partial_{v_{j}}(\sigma_{ij}b)+\zeta_{i}fb\Big)\partial_{v_{i}}\chi_{R}\\
&\quad+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}(b\mathscr{P}f+bS-f\mathscr{P}^{*}b)\chi_{R}.\end{split}

At last, to pass to the limit
R\to\infty
, we observe that the assumptions (
2.22
) imply

\displaystyle\forall R>1,\quad|\nabla_{v}\chi_{R}(v)|\leq\frac{6}{1+|v|}.

Combining this estimate with what we have in the assumptions (W1)–(L3)–(L4), we observe that the integrands on the right-hand side of (
2.23
) can be bounded by

\displaystyle|b\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{j}}f\partial_{v_{i}}\chi_{R}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b||\partial_{v_{j}}f|\lesssim|b\omega^{-1}|^{2}+|\omega\partial_{v_{j}}f|^{2}\lesssim|b\omega^{-1}|^{2}+|\nabla_{v}(f\omega)|^{2}+|f\omega|^{2},

\displaystyle|f\partial_{v_{j}}(\sigma_{ij}b)\partial_{v_{i}}\chi_{R}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|fb|+|f\sigma_{ij}\partial_{v_{j}}b\partial_{v_{i}}\chi_{R}|\lesssim|f\omega|^{2}+|b\omega^{-1}|^{2}+|\nabla_{v}(b\omega^{-1})|^{2},

\displaystyle|\zeta_{i}fb\partial_{v_{i}}\chi_{R}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|f\omega|^{2}+|b\omega^{-1}|^{2}.

For the integrand in the last line of (
2.23
), we know that
b\in L^{2}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}^{T})
,
\mathscr{P}f\in L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})
,
f\in L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})
, and
\mathscr{P}^{*}b\in L^{2}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}^{T})
. Therefore, the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem applies to (
2.23
) and we get

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f_{T}b_{T}-\iint_{\mathcal{O}}f_{0}b_{0}

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}Sb+\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}(b\mathscr{P}f-f\mathscr{P}^{*}b)

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}Sb.

This completes the proof.
∎

2.2.
Basic facts on spatially homogeneous and radial solutions to (
1.1
)

In this section we recall some properties of spatially homogeneous and radial solutions to (
1.1
). It is easy to check that if the initial datum
g_{0}(v)
is radial, then the solution
g_{t}(v)
to (
1.1
) is also radial at all times
t\geq 0
, and due to this symmetry the boundary condition is automatically satisfied. We may then view
g
as a solution to (
1.1
) in all of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Furthermore, owing to the conservation laws in (
1.3
), we have that

\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1,v,|v|^{2})g_{t}(v)\,\textnormal{d}v=0,

and consequently the coefficients
\rho[g],u[g],\theta[g]
in (
1.1
) are constant. In particular, since
g
is radial we obtain that
j[g]=u[g]=0
. Thus, if
\rho[g_{0}]=1
, then we simply have

\theta[g]=\frac{E[g_{0}]}{d}.

Therefore, under the assumptions of Theorem
I
,
g
is merely a solution to the linear Fokker–Planck equation

(2.24)

\partial_{t}g=\left(\frac{E[g_{0}]}{d}\Delta_{v}g+\nabla_{v}\cdot(vg)\right)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

In the following lemma we provide some elementary weighted
L^{\infty}
-estimates.

Lemma 2.5
.

We assume that
\omega\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
. Let
g_{t}
denote the solution to (
2.24
) corresponding to initial datum
g_{0}\in W^{2,\infty}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then there exists
\lambda>0
such that

\|g_{t}\|_{W^{2,\infty}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim e^{\lambda t}\|g_{0}\|_{W^{2,\infty}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Proof
. We only give the proof for the first derivative. The analogous estimate for the second derivative, and
g
itself, follows in the same way. Furthermore, in order to simplify the notations, we will just assume without loss of generality
E[g_{0}]=d
. Then
g_{t}
is a solution to

\partial_{t}g=\Delta_{v}g+\nabla_{v}\cdot(vg).

We denote
G_{t}:=\omega\nabla_{v}g_{t}
, and after a direct computation, we find that
G
is a solution to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}G=\Delta_{v}G+\left(v-2\frac{\nabla_{v}\omega}{\omega}\right)\cdot\nabla_{v}G+c_{\omega}G,

\displaystyle c_{\omega}:=\left(2\frac{|\nabla_{v}\omega|^{2}}{\omega}-\frac{\Delta_{v}\omega}{\omega}+2d-v\cdot\frac{\nabla_{v}\omega}{\omega}\right).

For
\omega\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
, we can straightforwardly check that
\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}c_{\omega}(v)<\infty
, and therefore the maximum principle provides
\|G_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq e^{c_{\omega}t}\|G_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.
∎

Second, we have the following trend-to-equilibrium result, whose proof we refer to
[
39
, Section 3]
.

Lemma 2.6
.

We assume that
\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{g_{0}}
. Then given
g_{0}\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\rho[g_{0}]=1
, there exist
a>0
and
C_{a}>0
such that

\|g_{t}(v)-\mu\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C_{a}e^{-at}\|g_{0}-\mu\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

3.
On the weakly inhomogeneous regime

3.1.
Setup of the problem

Let us derive the equation that is satisfied by
h:=f-g
. We recall
\eqref{eq: main}_{1}
here,

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f

\displaystyle=\rho[f]\Big(\theta[f]\Delta_{v}f+\nabla_{v}\cdot((v-u[f])f)\Big)

\displaystyle=\rho[f]\theta[f]\Delta_{v}f+d\rho[f]f+\rho[f](v-u[f])\cdot\nabla_{v}f,

and linearize the macroscopic coefficients with respect to
g
, by expanding everything up to the first order. First of all, we can write

\rho[f]=\rho[g_{0}]+\rho[h]=1+\rho[h].

Second, there holds

\displaystyle\rho[f]\theta[f]

\displaystyle=\rho[g]\theta[g]+\frac{1}{d}E[h]-\frac{|j[h]|^{2}}{d(\rho[g_{0}]+\rho[h])}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}E[g_{0}]+\frac{1}{d}E[h]-\frac{|j[h]|^{2}}{d(1+\rho[h])}.

Third, we have

\rho[f]u[f]=j[f]=j[h],

thanks to the radial symmetry of
g
(namely that
j[g]=0
). The equation for
h
can thus be written as

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})h

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{d}E[g_{0}]+\frac{1}{d}E[h]-\frac{|j[h]|^{2}}{d(1+\rho[h])}\right)\Delta_{v}h

\displaystyle\quad+\Big((1+\rho[h])v-j[h]\Big)\cdot\nabla_{v}h+dh+dg\rho[h]+dh\rho[h]

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{1}{d}E[h]-\frac{|j[h]|^{2}}{d(1+\rho[h])}\right)\Delta_{v}g+(\rho[h]v-j[h])\cdot\nabla_{v}g.

In order to simplify the notation, we define

\displaystyle{\bf A}[h](t,x):=\frac{|j[h]|^{2}}{1+\rho[h]},\quad{\bf B}[h](t,x,v):=\rho[h]v-j[h],

with which the equation for
h
can then be simply written as

(3.1)

\begin{split}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})h&=\frac{1}{d}\left(E[g_{0}]+E[h]-{\bf A}[h]\right)\Delta_{v}h+(v+{\bf B}[h])\cdot\nabla_{v}h+dh\\
&\quad+dg\rho[h]+dh\rho[h]+\frac{1}{d}\Delta_{v}g\,E[h]+{\bf B}[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}g\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{d}\Delta_{v}g\,{\bf A}[h].\end{split}

Towards the end of addressing the existence of a solution to (
3.1
), we consider first a regularized version of (
3.1
). The construction of a solution to (
3.1
) is surprisingly delicate, owing to the presence of quadratic nonlinear terms in the principal part of the operator. Therefore, the method of approximation has to be chosen carefully. We first define for
\varsigma>0
the usual family of mollifiers
({\bf r}_{\varsigma})
and
({\bf s}_{\varsigma})
, where
