\begin{split}&\textnormal{supp}({\bf r})\subset B_{1},\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\bf r}(x)\,\textnormal{d}x=1,\quad{\bf r}_{\varsigma}(x):=\frac{1}{\varsigma^{d}}{\bf r}\left(\frac{x}{\varsigma}\right),\\
&\textnormal{supp}({\bf s})\subset\left[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right],\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\bf s}(t)\,\textnormal{d}t=1,\quad{\bf s}_{\varsigma}(t):=\frac{1}{\varsigma}{\bf s}\left(\frac{t}{\varsigma}\right),\end{split}

here
B_{1}
denotes the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For a given function
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}:\Omega^{T}\to\mathbb{R}
, we then define its time-space regularized version
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}
as

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}(t,x):=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(s,y){\bf s}_{\varsigma}(t-s){\bf r}_{\varsigma}(x-2\varsigma n_{x}-y)\,\textnormal{d}s\textnormal{d}y.

Since the main difficulty comes from the presence of
{\bf A}[h]
as part of the diffusion coefficient, we now set

{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h]:=\frac{|j[h]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}|^{2}}{1+\rho[h]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}}.

The advantage of this formulation is that then, even if
\rho[h]
and
j[h]
are only known to converge for instance weakly
-*
, the regularized coefficient
{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h]
is at least pointwise convergent. Provided that we have some nice bounds on the macroscopic quantities, this pointwise convergence would then imply strong convergence in suitable Lebesgue spaces.

It turns out that the

The regularized equation in consideration then reads

(3.2)

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})h&=\frac{1}{d}\left(E[g_{0}]+E[h]-{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h]\right)\Delta_{v}h\\
&\quad+(v+{\bf B}[h])\cdot\nabla_{v}h+d(1+\rho[h])h\\
&\quad+dg\rho[h]+\frac{1}{d}\Delta_{v}gE[h]+{\bf B}[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}g-\frac{1}{d}{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h]\Delta_{v}g\end{aligned}&\text{in }\mathcal{O}^{T},\\
h|_{t=0}=h_{0}&\text{in }\mathcal{O},\\
\gamma_{-}h=\gamma_{+}h\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}&\text{on }\Sigma_{-}^{T}.\end{cases}

However, even the existence of a solution to (
3.2
) is yet nontrivial, so we approach using a linearized version of (
3.2
):

(3.3)

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})h&=\frac{1}{d}\left(E[g_{0}]+E[k]-{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\right)\Delta_{v}h\\
&\quad+(v+{\bf B}[k])\cdot\nabla_{v}h+d(1+\rho[k])h\\
&\quad+dg\rho[h]+\frac{1}{d}\Delta_{v}gE[h]+{\bf B}[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}g-\frac{1}{d}{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\Delta_{v}g\end{aligned}&\text{in }\mathcal{O}^{T},\\
h|_{t=0}=h_{0}&\text{in }\mathcal{O},\\
\gamma_{-}h=\gamma_{+}h\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}&\text{on }\Sigma_{-}^{T},\end{cases}

where we assume that
k=k(t,x,v)
is given from some set that we specify later. Let us remark that the solution to (
3.2
) is formally realized as a fixed point of the map
k\mapsto h
defined on (
3.3
).

Towards the analysis of (
3.3
), the following backwards equation (with zero source term and dual specular reflection) will also be utilized

(3.4)

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (-\partial_{t}-v\cdot\nabla_{x})b&=\frac{1}{d}\left(E[g_{0}]+E[k]-{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\right)\Delta_{v}b-(v+{\bf B}[k])\cdot\nabla_{v}b+d\rho[k]b\\
&\quad+d\rho[bg]+\frac{1}{d}|v|^{2}\rho[b\Delta_{v}g]+\rho[v\cdot b\nabla_{v}g]-v\rho[b\nabla_{v}g]\end{aligned}&\text{in }\mathcal{O}^{T},\\
b|_{t=T}=b_{T}&\text{in }\mathcal{O},\\
\gamma_{+}b=\gamma_{-}b\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}&\text{on }\Sigma_{+}^{T}.\end{cases}

In the weighted
L^{2}
framework, the well-posedness of both equations (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) follows as an application of Proposition
2.1
. For the forward equation (
3.3
), let us define the following wide range of polynomial weights which will act as a
reference class
:

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}:=\left\{\langle v\rangle^{m},\quad m>\frac{d}{2}+2\right\}.

We remark that
L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}\hookrightarrow L^{1}_{\langle v\rangle^{2}}
for every
\widetilde{\omega}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
because
\langle v\rangle^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

On the other hand, we remind the reader that the class of admissible weights is defined as

\mathcal{W}_{g_{0}}:=\left\{\begin{aligned} \langle v\rangle^{m},\quad&m>d+2,\\
\langle v\rangle^{m}e^{\gamma|v|^{\tau}},\quad&\text{either }m>0,\;\gamma>0,\text{ and }\tau\in(0,2)\\
&\text{or }m>0,\;\gamma\in\left(0,\frac{d}{2E[g_{0}]}\right),\text{ and }\tau=2\end{aligned}\right\}.

It is worth noting that the computation of
\Gamma
in (
2.1
) is relatively simple for the class of weights above. For instance, let us consider a diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{ij}=\sigma\delta_{ij}
with
\sigma=\sigma(t,x)
real-valued, which is enough for our purpose; then setting
\mathscr{L}:=\sigma\Delta_{v}+\zeta\cdot\nabla_{v}+\eta
, consider any weight of the form
\omega=\langle v\rangle^{m}e^{\gamma\langle v\rangle^{\tau}}
, with
m\geq 0
,
\gamma\geq 0
,
\tau\geq 0
so that in particular all weights in
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
or
\mathcal{W}_{g_{0}}
are taken into consideration. Then with the notation
W:=\log\omega
, (
2.1
) reduces to

(3.5)

\begin{split}\Gamma_{\mathscr{L},\omega,p}&=\sigma|\nabla_{v}W|^{2}+\left(\frac{2}{p}-1\right)\sigma\Delta_{v}W-\zeta\cdot\nabla_{v}W-\frac{1}{p}\nabla_{v}\cdot\zeta+\eta,\\
\nabla_{v}W&=\left(\frac{m}{\langle v\rangle^{2}}+\gamma\tau|v|^{\tau-2}\right)v,\\
\Delta_{v}W&=m\frac{d+(d-2)|v|^{2}}{\langle v\rangle^{4}}+\gamma\tau(d+\tau-2)|v|^{\tau-2}.\end{split}

For the remainder of this section, we now fix a specific weight

\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{g_{0}},

and use the following functional space corresponding to this weight: for some
a>0
, we will assume that
k
lies in

\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}:=\left\{k\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})\;\middle|\;\|k\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}\leq a\right\}.

3.2.
Analysis of the linear equation

Lemma 3.1
.

There exists
a_{1}>0
small enough, and independent of
\varsigma
, such that for any
a\leq a_{1}
and
k\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
, the following holds true: for any polynomial weight
\widetilde{\omega}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
, if

\displaystyle h_{0}\in L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O})\quad\text{and}\quad g_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{\langle v\rangle\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

then the linear problem (
3.3
) admits a unique solution
h\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O}))
.

Proof
. For the sake of clarity, let us set

(3.6)

\begin{split}\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]&:=\frac{1}{d}\left(E[g_{0}]+E[k]-{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\right),\\
\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k]h&:=\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\Delta_{v}h+(v+{\bf B}[k])\cdot\nabla_{v}h+d(1+\rho[k])h.\end{split}

We choose
a_{1}\leq 1/2
small enough (and independent of
\varsigma
) so that for any
a\leq a_{1}
, the membership
k\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
implies both

(3.7)

\begin{cases}|\rho[k]|\leq\frac{1}{2},\\
\frac{1}{2d}E[g_{0}]\leq\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\leq\frac{3}{2d}E[g_{0}]\end{cases}\quad\text{in }\Omega^{T}.

Indeed, such a choice is possible since
\rho[k]
,
j[k]
, and
E[k]
can be controlled pointwisely as

\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle v\rangle^{2}k\,\textnormal{d}v\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})}\leq\|\langle v\rangle^{2}\omega^{-1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|k\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}\leq\|\langle v\rangle^{2}\omega^{-1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,a,

whereas
{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]
can be controlled pointwisely, and uniformly in
\varsigma
, by using the relations

\displaystyle\|\rho[k]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star{\bf r}_{\varsigma}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})}\leq\|\rho[k]\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})},\quad\|j[k]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star{\bf r}_{\varsigma}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})}\leq\|j[k]\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})}.

Now, it is enough to check that the conditions of Proposition
2.1
are satisfied by the equation (
3.3
) and the operator set in (
3.6
). First, the diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]
is obviously symmetric and strictly positive definite, thanks to (
3.7
). Therefore (L1) is satisfied.

In the notation of (
3.5
), we can set

\zeta[k]:=v+{\bf B}[k]=(1+\rho[k])v-j[k],\quad\eta[k]:=d(1+\rho[k])

to find that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\widetilde{\omega},2}=\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\frac{|\nabla_{v}\widetilde{\omega}|^{2}}{\widetilde{\omega}^{2}}-(v+{\bf B}[k])\cdot\frac{\nabla_{v}\widetilde{\omega}}{\widetilde{\omega}}+\frac{d}{2}+d\rho[k].

Owing to (
3.7
),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\widetilde{\omega},2}(v)\leq\frac{3}{2d}E[g_{0}]\frac{|\nabla_{v}\widetilde{\omega}|^{2}}{\widetilde{\omega}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{v\cdot\nabla_{v}\widetilde{\omega}}{\widetilde{\omega}}+|j[k]|\left|\frac{\nabla_{v}\widetilde{\omega}}{\widetilde{\omega}}\right|+\frac{d}{2}+d|\rho[k]|.

Since
j[k],\rho[k]\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})
, we deduce that for any
\widetilde{\omega}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
, there exists
C>0
independent of
\varsigma
such that

\sup_{(t,x,v)\in\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\widetilde{\omega},2}\leq C.

Consequently (L2) holds. It is further straightforward to check that (L3) is satisfied by
\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k]
.

We now set the nonlocal operator of (
3.3
) as

(3.8)

\mathscr{P}h:=dg\rho[h]+\frac{1}{d}\Delta_{v}gE[h]+\nabla_{v}g\cdot{\bf B}[h].

The condition (P1) can then be checked through the following computations: by using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|g|^{2}|\rho[h]|^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\iint_{\Omega^{T}}|\rho[h]|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\|\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\|h\|_{L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2},

and

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|\Delta_{v}gE[h]|^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\iint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|E[h]|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\||v|^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\|h\|_{L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2},

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|\nabla_{v}g\cdot\rho[h]v|^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\langle v\rangle\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\|\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\|h\|_{L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2},

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|h||\nabla_{v}g\cdot j[h]|

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\langle v\rangle\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\|v\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\|h\|_{L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2}.

Finally, we set the source term in (
3.3
) as

S=S_{1}:=-\frac{1}{d}{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\Delta_{v}g,

from which we immediately have that

\|S_{1}\widetilde{\omega}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2}\lesssim\|{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})}^{2}\|\Delta_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}<+\infty.

This shows (S1). We conclude to the well-posedness of (
3.3
) thanks to Proposition
2.1
.
∎

Similarly we have the following result for the backward equation.

Lemma 3.2
.

For
a_{1}
as chosen in Lemma
3.1
, whenever
a\leq a_{1}
, then the following holds true: for any weight
\widetilde{\omega}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
, if

b_{T}\in L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}),\quad k\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a},\quad\text{and}\quad g_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{\langle v\rangle\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

then the backwards linear problem (
3.4
) admits a unique solution
b\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}))
.

Proof
. We note that the formal adjoint of
\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k]
is given by

\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k]^{*}b:=\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\Delta_{v}b-(v+{\bf B}[k])\cdot\nabla_{v}b+d\rho[k]b.

Therefore

\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k]^{*},\widetilde{\omega}^{-1},2}=\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\widetilde{\omega},2}.

Thus, the proof of Lemma
3.1
tells us that (L2) is satisfied by
\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k]^{*},\widetilde{\omega}^{-1},2}
. The remaining assertions are rather straightforward, thus let us only check that (P1) is satisfied by
\mathscr{P}^{*}
and the weight
\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}
. Recall that the dual projection operator of (
3.8
), as written in (
3.4
), is formally given by

(3.9)

\mathscr{P}^{*}b:=d\rho[bg]+\frac{1}{d}|v|^{2}\rho[b\Delta_{v}g]+\rho[v\cdot b\nabla_{v}g]-v\rho[b\nabla_{v}g].

Each term of
\mathscr{P}^{*}
can be shown to satisfy (P1) in the following manner: using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|\rho[bg]|^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{-2}

\displaystyle=\|\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\iint_{\Omega^{T}}|\rho[bg]|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|b\|_{L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2},

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|v|^{4}|\rho[b\Delta_{v}g]|^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{-2}

\displaystyle=\||v|^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\iint_{\Omega^{T}}|\rho[b\Delta_{v}g]|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\||v|^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|b\|_{L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2}\|\Delta_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}.

In the same way,

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|\rho[v\cdot b\nabla_{v}g]|^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{-2}

\displaystyle=\|\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\iint_{\Omega^{T}}|\rho[v\cdot b\nabla_{v}g]|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|b\|_{L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2}\|\nabla_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\langle v\rangle\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2},

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|v\rho[b\nabla_{v}g]|^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{-2}

\displaystyle=\|v\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\iint_{\Omega^{T}}|\rho[b\nabla_{v}g]|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|v\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|b\|_{L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}^{2}\|\nabla_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}.

We thus get that (P1) is satisfied by
\mathscr{P}^{*}
and the weight
\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}
. The well-posedness of (
3.4
) then follows from Proposition
2.1
.
∎

Next, we transition to the
L^{1}
framework of the backwards equation.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists
a_{2}>0
(dependent on the given weight
\omega
) such that if
a\leq\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and
k\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
, the following holds. Suppose that

(3.10)

g_{0}\in W^{2,\infty}_{\langle v\rangle\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

and for any weight
\widetilde{\omega}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
growing slowly enough so that
\widetilde{\omega}\omega^{-1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, let
b_{T}\in L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}(\mathcal{O})
, and denote by
b\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}(\mathcal{O}))
the unique solution to the backwards equation (
3.4
) as established in Lemma
3.2
. Then
b
satisfies the estimate

(3.11)

\forall t\in[0,T],\quad\|b_{t}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathcal{O})}\leq C\exp\Big(\lambda(T-t)\exp(CT)\Big)\|b_{T}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathcal{O})}

for some constants
C>0
and
\lambda>0
independent of both
\varsigma
and
T
.

Remark 3.4
.

For instance, if
\omega(v)=\langle v\rangle^{m}
where
m=d+2+\alpha
with
\alpha>0
, we take
\widetilde{\omega}(v)=\langle v\rangle^{2+(d+\alpha)/2}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
.

Remark 3.5
.

Thanks to the choice of
\widetilde{\omega}
, the assumption (
3.10
) guarantees
g_{0}\in W^{2,2}_{\langle v\rangle\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so that the results of Lemma
3.2
can be applied.

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.
First we remark that since
\widetilde{\omega}\omega^{-1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, Hölder’s inequality shows that the embedding

L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}\hookrightarrow L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}

is continuous. Therefore,
b_{T}\in L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}
and
b\in C([0,T];L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}})
, which means that the estimate (
3.11
) makes sense.

Let us define for
\delta>0
the approximations
\beta_{\delta}(z)=\sqrt{z^{2}-\delta^{2}}-\delta
, so that
\beta_{\delta}(z)\nearrow|z|
and
\beta_{\delta}(b)
is a distributional solution to

\displaystyle(-\partial_{t}-v\cdot\nabla_{x})\beta_{\delta}(b)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\Delta_{v}\beta_{\delta}(b)-\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\beta^{\prime\prime}(b)|\nabla_{v}b|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-(v+{\bf B}[k])\cdot\nabla_{v}\beta_{\delta}(b)+d\rho[k]\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(b)b+\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(b)\mathscr{P}^{*}b.

Here
\sigma[k]
is the diffusion coefficient defined in (
3.6
), and
\mathscr{P}^{*}
is the dual projection operator given in (
3.9
). Performing some elementary algebraic manipulations, we point out that the above is equivalent to

(3.12)

\begin{split}(-\partial_{t}-v\cdot\nabla_{x})\beta_{\delta}(b)&=\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\Delta_{v}\beta_{\delta}(b)-(v+{\bf B}[k])\cdot\nabla_{v}\beta_{\delta}(b)+d\rho[k]\beta_{\delta}(b)\\
&\quad-\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\beta_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}(b)|\nabla_{v}b|^{2}+d\rho[k](\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(b)b-\beta_{\delta}(b))+\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(b)\mathscr{P}^{*}b\\
&=\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k]^{*}b-\sigma[k]\beta_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}(b)|\nabla_{v}b|^{2}+d\rho[k](\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(b)b-\beta_{\delta}(b))+\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(b)\mathscr{P}^{*}b,\end{split}

here
\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k]
denoting the differential operator in (
3.6
).

To now perform
\omega^{-1}
-weighted estimates on
\beta_{\delta}(b)
, we observe that

\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k]\omega^{-1}=\omega^{-1}\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega,\infty},

where
\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega,\infty}
is defined as in (
3.5
), with
\zeta[k]=v+{\bf B}[k]=(1+\rho[k])v-j[k]
and
\eta[k]=d(1+\rho[k])
. We claim that for our given choice of
\omega\in\mathcal{W}
, there exists
a_{2}>0
small enough so that
a\leq\min\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
implies the existence of
C>0
independent of
\varsigma
such that

(3.13)

\omega^{-1}\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega,\infty}\leq C\omega^{-1}.

Indeed, let us write
\omega=\langle v\rangle^{m}e^{\gamma|v|^{\tau}}
. In the case where
\gamma=0
(
i.e.
when
\omega
is a polynomial), we merely have

\lim_{|v|\to\infty}\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega,\infty}(v)=-m(1+\rho[k])+d(1+\rho[k])<\infty.

In the case when
\tau\in(0,2)
, we have
\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega,\infty}\to-\infty
unconditionally. In the case where
\tau=2
and
\gamma\in(0,d/2E[g_{0}])
, the leading order of
\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega,\infty}
is given by (see (
3.5
))

\begin{split}\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega,\infty}&\sim\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]|\nabla_{v}\log\omega|^{2}-\zeta[k]\cdot\nabla_{v}\log\omega\\
&\sim 2\gamma\,(2\gamma\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]-(1+\rho[k]))|v|^{2}.\end{split}

Therefore
\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega,\infty}\to-\infty
if and only if

\frac{\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]}{1+\rho[k]}<\frac{1}{2\gamma}.

Since
\gamma\in(0,d/2E[g_{0}])
we can write

\frac{1}{2\gamma}=\frac{E[g_{0}]}{d}+\kappa_{0}

for some
\kappa_{0}>0
. Thus we just need to prove that, if
a_{2}
is small enough, then
a\leq a_{2}
and
k\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
imply

\frac{\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]}{1+\rho[k]}\leq\frac{E[g_{0}]}{d}+\frac{\kappa_{0}}{2}.

This follows by a standard computation because
\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\approx E[g_{0}]/d
and
1+\rho[k]\approx 1
for
k
small in the
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}
-norm. Hence, in all cases we have
\Gamma_{\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega,\infty}\leq C
and therefore (
3.13
) is proven.

Coming back the equation (
3.12
), we can first compute

(3.14)

\begin{split}-\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\beta_{\delta}(b_{t})\omega^{-1}&=\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\beta_{\delta}(b)\mathscr{L}_{\varsigma}[k]\omega^{-1}-\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\sigma[k]\beta_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}(b_{t})|\nabla_{v}b_{t}|^{2}\omega^{-1}\\
&\quad+\iint_{\mathcal{O}}d\rho[k](\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(b)b-\beta_{\delta}(b))\omega^{-1}+\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(b_{t})\mathscr{P}^{*}b_{t}\,\omega^{-1}\\
&=:\rm{I}+\rm{II}+\rm{III}+\rm{IV}.\end{split}

For
\rm{I}
we just use (
3.13
), and the fact that
\beta_{\delta}\geq 0
, to estimate

\rm{I}\leq C\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\beta_{\delta}(b)\omega^{-1}.

Next we throw away
\rm{II}
by using the facts that
\beta_{\delta}
is convex, and
\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]>0
(by (
3.7
)). For
\rm{III}
, we note that

|\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(b)b-\beta_{\delta}(b)|=\left|\frac{-\delta^{2}}{\sqrt{b^{2}+\delta^{2}}}+\delta\right|\leq 2\delta,

thus using that
\rho[k]\in L^{\infty}
and
\omega^{-1}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we can simply throw away
\rm{III}
later by taking
\delta\searrow 0
. Finally, we need to estimate
\rm{IV}
. Since
|\beta_{\delta}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 1
, it is at least clear that

(3.15)

\textrm{IV}\leq\iint_{\mathcal{O}}|\mathscr{P}^{*}b|\,\omega^{-1}.

Now, there are four terms in the definition of
\mathscr{P}^{*}
as written in (
3.9
). We will insert into the right-hand side of (
3.15
) each of those terms. For the first term, we can estimate

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}|\rho[bg]|\omega^{-1}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v^{\prime}}}b(v^{\prime})g(v^{\prime})\,\textnormal{d}v^{\prime}\right|\,\omega^{-1}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Omega}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|b_{t}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}}\|\omega^{-1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

thanks to the Fubini–Tonelli theorem. Here, we emphasize that the right-hand side is well-defined since we already know that
b\in C([0,T];L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}})
. For the second term, in the same way, we obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}|v|^{2}|\rho[b\Delta_{v}g]|\omega^{-1}\lesssim\|\Delta_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|b_{t}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}}\||v|^{2}\omega^{-1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where indeed
|v|^{2}\omega^{-1}\in L^{1}
thanks to
\omega\in\mathcal{W}
. The third and fourth terms also follow in a similar fashion:

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}|\rho[v\cdot b\nabla_{v}g]|\omega^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{\langle v\rangle\omega}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|b_{t}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}}\|\omega^{-1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}|v\rho[b\nabla_{v}g]|\omega^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{v}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|b_{t}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}}\|v\omega^{-1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

In all, we get from (
3.15
) and the estimates for
g
(Lemma
2.5
applied with the weight
\langle v\rangle\omega
) that there exists
\lambda>0
for which

\textrm{IV}\leq Ce^{\lambda T}\|b_{t}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}}\|\langle v\rangle^{2}\omega^{-1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Collecting the estimates, we conclude from (
3.14
) that

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\beta_{\delta}(b_{t})\omega^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\beta_{\delta}(b_{T})\omega^{-1}+O(\delta)+Ce^{\lambda T}\int_{t}^{T}\|b_{s}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}}\,\textnormal{d}s.

By the monotone convergence theorem we can pass to the limit
\delta\searrow 0
, obtaining

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}|b_{t}|\omega^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{\mathcal{O}}|b_{T}|\omega^{-1}+Ce^{\lambda T}\int_{t}^{T}\|b_{s}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathcal{O})}\,\textnormal{d}s.

We obtain the assertion of the lemma via Grönwall’s inequality.
∎

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
a
,
k
, and
g_{0}
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
3.3
. Then for each
h_{0}\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})
, there exists a unique solution
h\in C([0,T];L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})-*)
to the linear forwards problem (
3.3
). Furthermore, the solution satisfies

(3.16)

\forall t\in[0,T],\quad\|h_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq C_{1}\exp(\lambda T\exp(CT))\|h_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}+C_{2}\exp(\lambda T\exp(CT))a^{2}

for some generic constants
C,C_{1},C_{2}>0
which are independent of
\varsigma
and
T
.

Proof
. Fix any weight
\widetilde{\omega}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
so that
\widetilde{\omega}\omega^{-1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then by Hölder’s inequality, the embedding
L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})\hookrightarrow L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O})
is continuous. Therefore,
h_{0}\in L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O})
and we deduce via Lemma
3.1
that there exists a unique solution
h\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}})
to (
3.3
). Thus, it remains for us to prove that
h_{T}\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}
, namely the estimate (
3.16
), and also that
t\mapsto h_{t}\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}-*
is continuous. Toward this end, let
b_{T}\in L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}
and denote by
b\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}})
the unique solution to the backwards problem (
3.4
) corresponding to
b_{T}
. We then apply Lemma
2.3
to find that

(3.17)

\iint_{\mathcal{O}}h_{T}b_{T}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v=\iint_{\mathcal{O}}h_{0}b(0)-\frac{1}{d}\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\Delta_{v}g\,b_{t}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t.

Next, we utilize the Riesz representation theorem as follows: using that
L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}
is dense in
L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}
,

\begin{split}\|h_{T}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}&=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b_{T}\in L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}\\
\|b_{T}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\iint_{\mathcal{O}}h_{T}b_{T}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\\
&=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b_{T}\in L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}\\
\|b_{T}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left(\iint_{\mathcal{O}}h_{0}b(0)\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v-\frac{1}{d}\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\Delta_{v}g\,b_{t}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t\right)\\
&\leq C_{1}\|h_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\exp(\lambda T\exp(CT))\\
&\quad+C\|{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})}\exp(\lambda T)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(\lambda(T-t)\exp(CT)\Big)\,\textnormal{d}t\\
&\leq C_{1}\|h_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\exp(\lambda T\exp(CT))+C_{2}\exp(\lambda T\exp(CT))a^{2}\end{split}

for some constants
C,C_{1},C_{2}>0
. In the above, we used that
{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]
is uniformly bounded in
\varsigma
:

0\leq{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\leq\frac{\|j[k]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}}{1-\|\rho[k]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}\|_{L^{\infty}}}\leq\frac{\|j[k]\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}}{1-\|\rho[k]\|_{L^{\infty}}}\leq 2\|k\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}^{2}\lesssim a^{2},

as well as Lemma
2.5
(the
L^{\infty}
-estimates for
\omega\Delta_{v}g
). We conclude that
h_{T}\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}
and that (
3.16
) holds.

Finally, we can easily check that
h\in C([0,T];L^{\infty}_{\omega}-*)
. Since we already know that
h\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})
, let us merely prove that
t\mapsto\iint_{\mathcal{O}}(h_{t}\,\mathfrak{b})
is continuous for every
\mathfrak{b}\in L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}
. This is enough to conclude the claim, because
L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}}
is dense in
L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}
.

Let
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
, and denote by
b\in C([0,t];L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}})
the solution to the backwards problem (
3.4
) corresponding to terminal datum
b_{t}=\mathfrak{b}
. The duality formula (
3.17
) shows that

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}h_{t}\mathfrak{b}-\iint_{\mathcal{O}}h_{s}b_{s}

\displaystyle=-\int_{s}^{t}\iint_{\mathcal{O}}{\bf A}[k]\Delta_{v}g\,b_{\tau}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}\tau.

The above is equivalent to

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}(h_{t}-h_{s})\mathfrak{b}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{O}}h_{s}(b_{s}-\mathfrak{b})-\int_{s}^{t}\iint_{\mathcal{O}}{\bf A}[k]\Delta_{v}g\,b_{\tau}\,\textnormal{d}x\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}\tau.

On the one hand, since
b\in C([0,T];L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}})
, the first integral of the right-hand side satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\iint_{\mathcal{O}}h_{s}(b_{s}-\mathfrak{b})\right|\leq\left(\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\|h_{\tau}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}\right)\|b_{s}-\mathfrak{b}\|_{L^{1}_{\omega^{-1}}(\mathcal{O})}\xrightarrow[|s-t|\to 0]{}0.

On the other hand, the second integral of the right-hand side is absolutely continuous in
s
because the integrand is integrable over
[0,T]
. Therefore,

\forall\mathfrak{b}\in L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}},\quad\left|\iint_{\mathcal{O}}(h_{t}-h_{s})\mathfrak{b}\right|\xrightarrow[|s-t|\to 0]{}0,

which proves the claim.
∎

The following result is immediate

Corollary 3.7
.

Given
T>0
, there exists
a_{3}>0
(dependent on
T
) small enough such that for each
a<\min\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
, the following holds. For
g_{0}\in W^{2,\infty}_{\langle v\rangle\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
k\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
, let
(S_{k}(t,0))_{t\in[0,T]}
denote the family of evolution operators defined so that for each
h_{0}\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}
, the unique solution to (
3.3
) in
C([0,T];L^{\infty}-*)\cap L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})
is given by
S_{k}(t,0)h_{0}
. There exists
\delta=\delta(a,T)>0
independent of
\varsigma
such that whenever
\|h_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq\delta
, the map

\Psi:k\mapsto S_{k}(t,0)h_{0}

leaves
\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
invariant.

Proof
. We fix
a_{3}
small enough (in terms of
T
) so that for any
a\leq a_{3}
, the last term in (
3.16
) satisfies

\displaystyle C_{2}\exp(\lambda T\exp(CT))\,a^{2}<\frac{a}{2}.

Then, for any
a<\min\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
, we deduce from (
3.16
) that

\displaystyle\|S_{k}(t,0)h_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}<\|h_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}C_{1}\exp(\lambda T\exp(CT))+\frac{a}{2}.

For any such given
a
, if we choose
\delta=\delta(a,T)>0
so small that

\delta\,C_{1}\exp(\lambda T\exp(CT))<\frac{a}{2},

then we find that
\|h_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq\delta
implies

\|S_{k}(t,0)h_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})}<a.

∎

3.3.
Global existence of the regularized equation

We now return to the discussion of (
3.2
).

Proposition 3.8
.

Following the notations of Corollary
3.7
, we assume that
a<\min\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
,
\|h_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq\delta(a,T)
, and also
g_{0}\in W^{2,\infty}_{\langle v\rangle\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then for each
\varsigma>0
, there exists at least one weak and renormalized solution
h^{\varsigma}\in C([0,T];L^{\infty}-*)\cap\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
to the regularized nonlinear problem (
3.2
).

Proof
. Thanks to Corollary
3.7
, we know that

\Psi:k\mapsto S_{k}(t,0)h_{0}

maps
\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
into itself. We endow
\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
with the
L^{\infty}_{\omega}-*
topology, for which it is obviously compact and convex. Hence, to conclude to the proposition, it only remains prove that
\Psi
is continuous with respect to this topology. In other words, let
k^{n}\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
denote a sequence with

k^{n}\rightharpoonup k\quad\text{in}\quad L^{\infty}_{\omega}-*.

For brevity, we write
h^{n}:=S_{k^{n}}(t,0)h_{0}
. We need to prove that
h^{n}\rightharpoonup S_{k}(t,0)h_{0}
.

(Step 1: Convergence of the coefficients.)

Let us first establish how the coefficients in
k^{n}
of (
3.3
) (such as
E[k^{n}]
,
{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k^{n}]
) behave with respect to weak
-*
convergence of
k
. Using the fact that
k^{n}\rightharpoonup k
in
L^{\infty}_{\omega}-*
, we can easily establish all of the following convergences

(3.18)

\rho[k^{n}]\rightharpoonup\rho[k],\quad j[k^{n}]\rightharpoonup j[k],\quad E[k^{n}]\rightharpoonup E[k]\quad\text{in}\quad L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})-*.

For instance, for
\varphi\in L^{1}(\Omega^{T})
there holds

\displaystyle\left|\iint_{\Omega^{T}}(E[k^{n}]-E[k])\varphi(t,x)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|v|^{2}(k^{n}-k)\varphi(t,x)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Big((k^{n}-k)\omega\Big)\,\Big(|v|^{2}\omega^{-1}\varphi(t,x)\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

We can now prove the strong convergence of
{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k^{n}]
. Indeed, let us observe first that the weak
-*
convergence of
j[k^{n}]
provides

\displaystyle\forall(t,x)\in\Omega^{T},\quad j[k^{n}]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\iint_{\Omega^{T}}j[k^{n}](s,y)\,{\bf s}_{\varsigma}(t-s){\bf r}_{\varsigma}(x-2\varsigma n_{x}-y)\,\textnormal{d}s\textnormal{d}y

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\iint_{\Omega^{T}}j[k](s,y)\,{\bf s}_{\varsigma}(t-s){\bf r}_{\varsigma}(x-2\varsigma n_{x}-y)\,\textnormal{d}s\textnormal{d}y

\displaystyle=j[k]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\zeta}(t,x).

Similarly we get
\rho[k^{n}]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}\to\rho[k]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}
pointwisely in
\Omega^{T}
. By the very definition of
{\bf A}_{\varsigma}
, we deduce

\forall(t,x)\in\Omega^{T},\quad{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k^{n}](t,x)\to{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k](t,x).

Since
({\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k^{n}])_{n}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})
, the convergence theorem of Vitali then provides

(3.19)

\forall p\in[1,\infty),\quad{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k^{n}]\to{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\quad\text{in}\quad L^{p}(\Omega^{T}).

In similar nature, we can easily prove that

(3.20)

{\bf B}[k^{n}]\rightharpoonup{\bf B}[k]\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}^{T}).

(Step 2: Passing to the limit in the PDE.) We now have enough information to pass to the limit. Indeed, consider the equation (
3.3
) satisfied by the
h^{n}
. We note that the diffusion coefficient (
=:\sigma_{\varsigma}[k^{n}]
) is strictly and uniformly lower bounded as a result of (
3.7
). Consequently, noting that
L^{\infty}_{\omega}\hookrightarrow L^{2}
, we can apply the renormalized Green’s formula (with the equation (
3.3
) satisfied by the
h^{n}
) to deduce that

(\sigma_{\varsigma}[h^{n}]h^{n})\quad\text{is bounded in}\quad L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v}.

In particular the uniform lower bound on
\sigma_{\varsigma}[k^{n}]
yields that

(3.21)

(\nabla_{v}h^{n})
is bounded in
L^{2}_{t,x,v}
.

Now denote by
\chi_{R,1}(x)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
a cutoff function satisfying

\chi_{R,1}(x)=1\quad\text{in}\quad\{x\in\Omega\;|\;\textnormal{dist}(x,\Omega^{c})>1/R\}.

Furthermore, consider a smooth radial cutoff function
\chi_{R,2}(v)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, satisfying
\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq R}(v)\leq\chi_{R,2}(v)\leq\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq 2R}(v)
.
Denoting
h^{n}_{R}(t,x,v):=h^{n}(t,x,v)\chi_{R,1}(x)\chi_{R,2}(v)
, we can derive from
\eqref{S: E: Lin}_{1}
the PDE that is satisfied by
h^{n}_{R}
, which holds in all of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}
because of the cutoffs. Then keeping in mind (
3.21
), we can apply the hypoelliptic energy estimates of
[
6
, Theorem 1.3]
1
1

1

Following the notation of Bouchut
[
6
]
, we take
p=2
,
r=0
,
\beta=1
,
\Omega=1
, and
m=2
, so that
s=1/4
.
, to deduce that
(h^{n}_{R})_{n}
is bounded in
H^{1/4}_{t,x}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v})
. Since

\chi_{R,1}(x)\chi_{R,2}(v)\equiv 1\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{O}_{R}:=\{(x,v)\in\Omega\;|\;\textnormal{dist}(x,\Omega^{c})>1/R,\;|v|<R\},

we consequently obtain that
(h^{n})
is bounded in
H^{1/4}_{t,x}([0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{R})
. We already mentioned that
(\nabla_{v}h^{n})
is bounded in
L^{2}_{t,x,v}
, so altogether

(h^{n})\quad\text{is bounded in}\quad H^{1/4}([0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{R}).

The usual diagonal argument proves that, up to some subsequence, there is a limit
\mathfrak{h}
such that

h^{n}\to\mathfrak{h}\quad\text{strongly in}\quad L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\mathcal{O})\quad\text{and a.e.}

Since
(h^{n})\subset\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
, an interpolation shows next that

(3.22)

\forall p\in[1,\infty),\quad h^{n}\to\mathfrak{h}\quad\text{strongly in}\quad L^{p}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\mathcal{O}).

The convergences in (
3.18
)–(
3.19
)–(
3.20
)–(
3.22
) allow us to pass to the limit in the equation
\eqref{S: E: Lin}_{1}
satisfied by the
h^{n}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\mathcal{O})
. We deduce that the limit
\mathfrak{h}
is a solution to
\eqref{S: E: Lin}_{1}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\mathcal{O})
corresponding to
k
.

(Step 3: Recovering the traces.) We first point out that clearly, at least up to some subsequence,
h^{n}\rightharpoonup\mathfrak{h}
in both
L^{\infty}_{\omega}-*
and
L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v,{\rm loc}}
, thanks to the uniqueness of distributional limits. Consequently
\mathfrak{h}
, being a distributional solution to the linear problem
\eqref{S: E: Lin}_{1}
and also a member of
L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v,{\rm loc}}
, is automatically a renormalized solution which admits traces
\gamma\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{t}
, as a straightforward adaptation of
[
41
, Theorem 4.2]
.

It only remains to prove that
\gamma\mathfrak{h}
satisfies the specular reflection boundary condition. Indeed, if so, then the well-posedness result of Proposition
2.1
provides that
\mathfrak{h}=S_{k}(t,0)h_{0}
in the class
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O}))
and therefore a.e. This would next imply, thanks to the uniqueness of
S_{k}(t,0)h_{0}
, that in fact the full sequence
(h^{n})
converges to
\mathfrak{h}=S_{k}(t,0)h_{0}
and the continuity of
\Psi
would be proven.

To establish that the boundary condition holds for
\mathfrak{h}
, we proceed exactly as in (Step 3) of the proof of Proposition
2.1
. Namely, recall once more that since
(h^{n})
is bounded in
L^{\infty}_{\omega}
(being members of
\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
), the family
(h^{n})
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O}))
; thus, by using the test function
\chi(x,v)=(n_{x}\cdot v)\widetilde{\omega}^{2}\langle v\rangle^{-4}
against the equation satisfied by
|h^{n}|^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\gamma h^{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma^{T},(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\widetilde{\omega}^{2}\langle v\rangle^{-4}\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t)}^{2}\leq C.

Then in the same way as in the proof of Proposition
2.1
, we obtain that
\gamma_{\pm}h^{n}\rightharpoonup\gamma_{\pm}\mathfrak{h}
weakly in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma^{T}_{\pm},(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t)
(thanks to uniqueness of the trace). Since each
\gamma h^{n}
satisfies
\gamma_{-}h^{n}=\gamma_{+}h^{n}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}
, we can write this against a test function and then pass to the limit to conclude that
\gamma_{-}\mathfrak{h}=\gamma_{+}\mathfrak{h}\circ\mathscr{V}_{x}
a.e. This completes the proof.
∎

3.4.
Passing to the limit in the regularized equation

For each
\varsigma>0
, we are armed with the existence of a solution to the regularized problem (
3.2
). In this section we will pass to the limit
\varsigma\searrow 0
and prove that the limit point is a solution to the original nonlinear equation (
3.1
).

Proposition 3.9
.

Let any
a<\min\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
be given, and assume
g_{0}\in W^{2,\infty}_{\langle v\rangle\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\|h_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}}\leq\delta(a,T)
. Denote by
(h^{\varsigma})_{\varsigma>0}\subset\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
a family of solutions to (
3.2
), as constructed in Proposition
3.8
. Then as
\varsigma\searrow 0
there is a weak
-*
limit point
h\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}\cap L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x,v}\cap L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v}
which is a weak and renormalized solution to the nonlinear problem (
3.1
).

Proof
. Being realized as a fixed point of the linear problem, we know that each
h^{\varsigma}\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
is a weak and renormalized solution to (
3.2
), and clearly there is a limit
h\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
such that

h^{\varsigma}\rightharpoonup h\quad\text{in}\quad L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}^{T})-*.

(Step 1: Uniform-in-
\varsigma
estimates.)
Let us find some estimates that are uniform in
\varsigma
. From the fact that
(h^{\varsigma})\subset\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
, it is first clear that

(3.23)

|\rho[h^{\varsigma}]|,\;|j[h^{\varsigma}]|,\;|E[h^{\varsigma}]|\lesssim a.

In particular, we can also obtain

(3.24)

0\leq{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h^{\varsigma}]\leq\frac{\|j[h^{\varsigma}]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}}{1-\|\rho[h^{\varsigma}]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}\|_{L^{\infty}}}\leq\frac{\|j[h]\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}}{1-\|\rho[h^{\varsigma}]\|_{L^{\infty}}}\leq Ca^{2},

thus
({\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h^{\varsigma}])
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(\Omega^{T})
. Similarly, it is clear that

(3.25)

({\bf B}[h^{\varsigma}]):=(\rho[h^{\varsigma}]v-j[h^{\varsigma}])\quad\text{is bounded in}\quad L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega;L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

Next, using the renormalized Green’s formula, we observe that the following holds, as can be seen by computing for instance as in (
2.2
):

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathcal{O}}|h^{\varsigma}|^{2}+\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\sigma_{\varsigma}[h^{\varsigma}]|\nabla_{v}h^{\varsigma}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\frac{d}{2}(1+\rho[h^{\varsigma}])|h^{\varsigma}|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\left(dg\rho[h^{\varsigma}]+\frac{1}{d}\Delta_{v}g\,E[h^{\varsigma}]+{\bf B}[h^{\varsigma}]\cdot\nabla_{v}g-\frac{1}{d}{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h^{\varsigma}]\Delta_{v}g\right)h^{\varsigma}.

In the above, we noted that the boundary integral vanishes by symmetry, and we remind the reader that
\sigma_{\varsigma}
is defined in (
3.6
). It is also worth emphasizing that all of the integrals above make sense because
L^{\infty}_{\omega}\hookrightarrow L^{2}
. All terms in the last integral above can easily be crudely bounded. For instance

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}g\rho[h^{\varsigma}]h^{\varsigma}

\displaystyle\lesssim Ca(\|g\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|h^{\varsigma}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}),

and

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}}{\bf B}[h^{\varsigma}]\cdot\nabla_{v}g\,h^{\varsigma}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{O}}(\rho[h^{\varsigma}]v-j[h^{\varsigma}])\cdot\nabla_{v}g\,h^{\varsigma}

\displaystyle=-\iint_{\mathcal{O}}\rho[h^{\varsigma}](dh^{\varsigma}+v\cdot\nabla_{v}h^{\varsigma})g

\displaystyle\lesssim a\|h^{\varsigma}\|_{L^{2}}\|g\|_{L^{2}}+a\|vg\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla_{v}h^{\varsigma}\|_{L^{2}},

by applying the Gauss–Ostrogradsky theorem. Note that the last term can be absorbed through Young’s inequality. In conclusion, we can obtain that
(h^{\varsigma})
is bounded in
L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{t,x}\cap L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v}
, especially thanks to the uniform nondegeneracy of
\sigma_{\varsigma}
(see (
3.7
)).

At last, using these bounds for
(h^{\varsigma})
just established, we can obtain the same energy estimate on the trace by computing as in (
2.19
). Namely, using
\varphi_{R}(x,v)=(v\cdot n_{x})\chi_{R}(v)
as a test function with
\chi_{R}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{R+1})
and
\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq R}\leq\chi_{R}\leq\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq R+1}
, we may obtain

(3.26)

\begin{split}\iiint_{[0,T]\times\partial\Omega\times B_{R}}|\gamma h^{\varsigma}|^{2}(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}&\leq C_{R}\Big(\|h^{\varsigma}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2})}^{2}+\|h_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|h^{\varsigma}\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v}}^{2}\Big)\\
&\leq C_{R},\end{split}

where the first inequality follows by resorting to the bounds in (
3.23
)–(
3.24
)–(
3.25
).

(Step 2: Passing to the limit and obtaining a weak solution to (
3.1
).) We now aim to pass to the limit in the Green’s formula of (
3.2
) satisfied by
(h^{\varsigma},\gamma h^{\varsigma})
. First we observe that just as in (Step 2) of the proof of Proposition
3.8
, we may apply the hypoelliptic estimates of
[
6
]
on a suitably truncated sequence of
h^{\varsigma}
, to deduce

\displaystyle h^{\varsigma}\to h\quad\text{strongly in}\quad L^{2}_{\rm loc}((0,T)\times\mathcal{O}).

Passing to subsequences, then arguing diagonally, we obtain a subsequence such that

h^{\varsigma}\to h\quad\text{a.e. in}\quad(0,T)\times\mathcal{O}.

The dominated convergence theorem of Vitali, along with the bounds in (
3.23
), then show

\rho[h^{\varsigma}]\to\rho[h],\quad j[h^{\varsigma}]\to j[h],\quad E[h^{\varsigma}]\to E[h]\quad\text{in}\quad L^{p}(\Omega^{T}),\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty).

It is also clear that

\displaystyle\rho[h^{\varsigma}]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}-\rho[h]

\displaystyle=(\rho[h^{\varsigma}]-\rho[h])*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}+(\rho[h]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}-\rho[h])

\displaystyle\to 0\quad\text{in}\quad L^{p}(\Omega^{T}),\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty),

and similarly
j[h^{\varsigma}]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}\to j[h]
in
L^{p}(\Omega^{T})
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
. Consequently, up to further subsequences,

\displaystyle{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h^{\varsigma}]\to{\bf A}[h],\quad{\bf B}[h^{\varsigma}]\to{\bf B}[h]\quad\text{a.e. and in}\quad L^{p}((0,T)\times\Omega;L^{p}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty).

The convergence of
{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h^{\varsigma}]
implies in particular that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\varsigma}[h^{\varsigma}]\to\sigma[h]:=\frac{1}{d}\left(E[g_{0}]+E[h]-{\bf A}[h]\right)\quad\text{a.e. and in}\quad L^{p}(\Omega^{T}),\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty).

On the other hand, the convergence of
{\bf B}[h^{\varsigma}]
implies

\displaystyle\zeta[h^{\varsigma}]

\displaystyle:=v+{\bf B}[h^{\varsigma}]

\displaystyle\to v+{\bf B}[h]=\zeta[h]\quad\text{in}\quad L^{p}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}}),\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty).

Next, we have

\displaystyle\eta[h^{\varsigma}]

\displaystyle:=d(1+\rho[h^{\varsigma}])

\displaystyle\to d(1+\rho[h])=\eta[h]\quad\text{in}\quad L^{p}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}}),\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty).

Similarly, denoting the remaining terms (last line) of (
3.2
) as

\displaystyle P_{\varsigma}[h^{\varsigma}]:=dg\rho[h^{\varsigma}]+\frac{1}{d}\Delta_{v}gE[h^{\varsigma}]+{\bf B}[h^{\varsigma}]\cdot\nabla_{v}g-\frac{1}{d}\Delta_{v}g\,{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[h^{\varsigma}],

the previously established convergences imply

\displaystyle P_{\varsigma}[h^{\varsigma}]

\displaystyle\to P[h]

\displaystyle:=dg\rho[h]+\frac{1}{d}\Delta_{v}gE[h]+{\bf B}[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}g-\frac{1}{d}\Delta_{v}g\,{\bf A}[h]

in
L^{p}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
, again for all
p\in[1,\infty)
. Finally, for the trace, from the uniform estimate (
3.26
) we know that there is a limit
\widetilde{\gamma}
for which (up to a subsequence)

\gamma h^{\varsigma}\,(n_{x}\cdot v)\rightharpoonup\widetilde{\gamma}\quad\text{weakly in }L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We then define
\overline{\gamma}:=\widetilde{\gamma}/(n_{x}\cdot v)
with the convention
\overline{\gamma}=0
when
n_{x}\cdot v=0
.

Collecting all of the convergences above, we may write the Green’s formula of (
3.2
) that is satisfied by
(h^{\varsigma},\gamma h^{\varsigma})
then pass to the limit, to obtain that
(h,\overline{\gamma})
satisfies the Green’s formula of (
3.1
). By the uniqueness of the trace we deduce
\overline{\gamma}=\gamma h
. It is clear that
\gamma h
, being the weak limit of
\gamma h^{\varsigma}
, satisfies the specular reflection boundary condition because each
\gamma h^{\varsigma}
does.

(Step 3: The solution is renormalized.) We consider the
(x,v)
-regularized functions
h_{\varepsilon}:=h\star_{\varepsilon}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}*_{v}{\bf r}_{\varepsilon}
as was done in the proof of Lemma
2.3
. Since
h\in L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v}\cap L^{\infty}_{\omega}
, it clearly holds that (up to a subsequence)

(3.27)

\begin{split}&h_{\varepsilon t}\to h_{t}\quad\text{a.e. and in}\quad L^{p}(\mathcal{O})\quad\forall p\in[2,\infty),\\
&h_{\varepsilon}\to h\quad\text{a.e. and in}\quad L^{p}((0,T)\times\mathcal{O})\quad\forall p\in[2,\infty),\\
&\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}\to\nabla_{v}h\quad\text{a.e. and in}\quad L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega;L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).\end{split}

Interpolating this with the fact that
h\in\mathcal{X}_{T,\omega,a}
, we obtain also that

(3.28)

\rho[h_{\varepsilon}]\to\rho[h],\quad j[h_{\varepsilon}]\to j[h],\quad E[h_{\varepsilon}]\to E[h]\quad\text{a.e. and in}\quad L^{p}(\Omega^{T})\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty),

and consequently

(3.29)

\begin{split}&\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\to\sigma[h],\quad\eta[h_{\varepsilon}]\to\eta[h]\quad\text{in}\quad L^{p}(\Omega^{T}),\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty),\\
&\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}]\to\zeta[h]\quad\text{in}\quad L^{p}([0,T]\times\Omega;L^{p}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty).\end{split}

Also, we have that
h_{\varepsilon}
is a solution to

(3.30)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}h_{\varepsilon}&=-(v\cdot\nabla_{x}h)_{\varepsilon}+\nabla_{v}\cdot(\sigma[h]\nabla_{v}h)_{\varepsilon}\\
&\quad+(\zeta[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}h)_{\varepsilon}+(\eta[h]h)_{\varepsilon}+(P[h])_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}}),\end{split}

and from this very equation
h_{\varepsilon}\in W^{1,1}((0,T);W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}))
. By Sobolev embedding we get a version of
h_{\varepsilon}
that is continuous on all of
(0,T)\times\overline{\mathcal{O}}
. It is clear that
h_{\varepsilon}
thus admits traces
h_{\varepsilon t}
and
\gamma h_{\varepsilon}
for every
t\in(0,T)
.

We now rewrite (
3.30
) into the more convenient form

(3.31)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}h_{\varepsilon}&=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}h_{\varepsilon}+\nabla_{v}\cdot(\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon})\\
&\quad+\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}]\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}+\eta[h_{\varepsilon}]h_{\varepsilon}+(P[h])_{\varepsilon}+\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon},\end{split}

where the commutator term is

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=-\Big((v\cdot\nabla_{x})h_{\varepsilon}-(v\cdot\nabla_{x}h)_{\varepsilon}\Big)+(\zeta[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}h)_{\varepsilon}-\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}]\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\quad+(\eta[h]h)_{\varepsilon}-\eta[h_{\varepsilon}]h_{\varepsilon}+\nabla_{v}\cdot\Big((\sigma[h]\nabla_{v}h)_{\varepsilon}-\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}\Big).

Let us put
\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}=\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{1}+\nabla_{v}\cdot\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
so that
\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{2}:=(\sigma[h]\nabla_{v}h)_{\varepsilon}-\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}
. To make clear how
\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}
vanishes in the limit, we write

(3.32)

\begin{split}\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{1}&=-\Big((v\cdot\nabla_{x})h_{\varepsilon}-(v\cdot\nabla_{x}h)_{\varepsilon}\Big)\\
&\quad+\Big((\zeta[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}h)_{\varepsilon}-\zeta[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}h\Big)+\Big(\zeta[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}h-\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}]\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}\Big)\\
&\quad+\Big((\eta[h]h)_{\varepsilon}-\eta[h]h\Big)+\Big(\eta[h]h-\eta[h_{\varepsilon}]h_{\varepsilon}\Big).\end{split}

On the right-hand side of (
3.32
), as
\varepsilon\searrow 0
we have that the first line tends to zero in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
by
[
17
, Lemma II.1]
. For the second line, its first term clearly tends to zero in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
by standard property of the mollifier. For its second term, we can rewrite again

\displaystyle\zeta[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}h-\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}]\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=(\zeta[h]-\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}])\cdot\nabla_{v}h+\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}]\cdot(\nabla_{v}h-\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}),

where on the one hand,
(\zeta[h]-\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}])\cdot\nabla_{v}h\to 0
in
L^{p}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
for every
p\in[1,\infty)
thanks to (
3.29
) and the fact that
\nabla_{v}h\in L^{2}
. On the other hand
\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}]\cdot(\nabla_{v}h-\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon})\to 0
in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
because we can combine
\eqref{S: E: hve itself}_{3}
with the fact that
|\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}]|=|v+{\bf B}[h_{\varepsilon}]|\lesssim 1+|v|
. The last line of
\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{1}
can be checked in the same way, and we obtain that
\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{1}\to 0
at least in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
.

Regarding
\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
, by rewriting

\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{2}=\Big((\sigma[h]\nabla_{v}h)_{\delta}-\sigma[h]\nabla_{v}h\Big)+\Big(\sigma[h]\nabla_{v}h-\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}\Big),

its first term clearly tends to zero in
L^{2}
, by standard property of the mollifier. The second term converges to zero in
L^{q}([0,T]\times\Omega;L^{q}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for any
q\in[1,2)
, because

\displaystyle\|\sigma[h]\nabla_{v}h-\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{q}_{t,x}L^{q}_{v,{\rm loc}}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sigma[h]\Big(\nabla_{v}h-\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}\Big)\right\|_{L^{q}_{t,x}L^{q}_{v,{\rm loc}}}+\left\|\Big(\sigma[h]-\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\Big)\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{q}_{t,x}L^{q}_{v,{\rm loc}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\sigma[h]\|_{L^{\infty}_{t,x}}\|\nabla_{v}h-\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{q}_{t,x}L^{q}_{v,{\rm loc}}}+\|\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]-\sigma[h]\|_{{L^{\frac{2q}{2-q}}_{t,x}}L^{\frac{2q}{2-q}}_{v,{\rm loc}}}\|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}_{t,x,v}},

and the right-hand side tends to zero due to (
3.27
)–(
3.29
). Altogether, we have established that

\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}\to 0\quad\text{in}\quad L^{2}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})+L^{q}([0,T]\times\Omega;W^{-1,q}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\quad\forall q\in[1,2).

Now recalling the regularity of
h_{\varepsilon}
, note that the chain rule applies, so for any
\beta\in C^{2}\cap W^{2,\infty}
we obtain that

(3.33)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\beta(h_{\varepsilon})+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta(h_{\varepsilon})&=\nabla_{v}\cdot(\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\nabla_{v}\beta(h_{\varepsilon}))-\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\\
&\quad+\zeta[h_{\varepsilon}]\cdot\nabla_{v}\beta(h_{\varepsilon})+\beta^{\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\Big(\eta[h_{\varepsilon}]h_{\varepsilon}+(P[h])_{\varepsilon}+\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{1}\Big)\\
&\quad+\nabla_{v}\cdot(\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{2}\beta^{\prime}(h_{\varepsilon}))-\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{D}^{\prime}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}}).\end{split}

Since
\beta\in W^{2,\infty}
, (
3.27
) implies

(3.34)

\beta(h_{\varepsilon})\to\beta(h),\quad\beta^{\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\to\beta^{\prime}(h),\quad\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\to\beta^{\prime\prime}(h)\quad\text{in}\quad L^{p}_{\rm loc}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})\quad\forall p\in[1,\infty).

Now using the convergences in (
3.27
)–(
3.28
)–(
3.29
)–(
3.34
), we can pass to the limit
\varepsilon\searrow 0
in (
3.33
). For instance let us demonstrate the convergence of just a few of the terms in (
3.33
). Writing

(3.35)

\begin{split}&\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\beta^{\prime\prime}(h)\sigma[h]|\nabla_{v}h|\\
&=\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}](|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-|\nabla_{v}h|^{2})+|\nabla_{v}h|^{2}(\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]-\beta^{\prime\prime}(h)\sigma[h]),\end{split}

the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.35
) converges to zero in
L^{1}_{t,x,v}
because

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]|\Big|\,|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-|\nabla_{v}h|^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle=\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]|(|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}|+|\nabla_{v}h|)\Big|\,|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}|-|\nabla_{v}h|\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}(|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\nabla_{v}h|^{2})\right)^{1/2}\left(\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\Big|\,|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}|-|\nabla_{v}h|\Big|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}(|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\nabla_{v}h|^{2})\right)^{1/2}\left(\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}-\nabla_{v}h|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}|\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon}-\nabla_{v}h|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\to 0.

Here we used the fact that
\|\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
. The second term on the right-hand side of (
3.35
) converges to zero in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\mathcal{O})
because
\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\to\beta^{\prime\prime}(h)\sigma[h]
a.e. implies that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\sigma[h_{\varepsilon}]\rightharpoonup\beta^{\prime\prime}(h)\sigma[h]
in
L^{\infty}-*
, which allows us to conclude using the fact that
|\nabla_{v}h|^{2}\in L^{1}
. As another example, the term
\nabla_{v}\cdot(\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{2}\beta^{\prime}(h_{\varepsilon}))
in (
3.33
) vanishes in the limit since for any test function
\chi\in\mathcal{D}((0,T)\times\mathcal{O})
, we have

\displaystyle\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\nabla_{v}\cdot(\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{2}\beta^{\prime}(h_{\varepsilon}))\chi

\displaystyle=-\iiint_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{2}\beta^{\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\nabla_{v}\cdot\chi\to 0

owing to the fact that
(\beta^{\prime}(h_{\varepsilon})\nabla_{v}\cdot\chi)
is bounded in
L^{\infty}
. In conclusion, taking the limit yields that
\beta(h)
solves, at least in the sense of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}([0,T]\times\mathcal{O})
:

(3.36)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\beta(h)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\beta(h)&=\nabla_{v}\cdot(\sigma[h]\nabla_{v}\beta(h))-\beta^{\prime\prime}(h)\sigma[h]|\nabla_{v}h|^{2}\\
&\quad+\zeta[h]\cdot\nabla_{v}\beta(h)+\beta^{\prime}(h)\Big(\eta[h]h+P[h]\Big)-\beta^{\prime\prime}(h)\nabla_{v}h.\end{split}

In order to recover the trace of
\beta(h)
, we briefly go back to the weak formulation (
3.31
) satisfied by
h_{\varepsilon}
. Our goal is to first prove the strong convergence
\gamma h_{\varepsilon}\to\gamma h
. Consider
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}>0
and the equation satisfied by
(h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-h_{\varepsilon_{1}})^{2}
:

(3.37)

\begin{split}&(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})(h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-h_{\varepsilon_{1}})^{2}\\
&=\sigma[h_{\varepsilon_{1}}]\Delta_{v}(h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-h_{\varepsilon_{1}})^{2}-2\sigma[h_{\varepsilon_{1}}]|\nabla_{v}(h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-h_{\varepsilon_{1}})|^{2}\\
&\quad+2(h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-h_{\varepsilon_{1}})(\sigma[h_{\varepsilon_{2}}]-\sigma[h_{\varepsilon_{1}}])\Delta_{v}h_{\varepsilon_{2}}\\
&\quad+\zeta[h_{\varepsilon_{1}}]\cdot\nabla_{v}(h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-h_{\varepsilon_{1}})^{2}+(\zeta[h_{\varepsilon_{2}}]-\zeta[h_{\varepsilon_{1}}])\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{\varepsilon_{2}}\\
&\quad+2(h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-h_{\varepsilon_{1}})\Big((P[h])_{\varepsilon_{2}}-(P[h])_{\varepsilon_{1}}+\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon_{2}}^{1}-\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{1}+\nabla_{v}\cdot(\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon_{2}}^{2}-\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{2})\Big).\end{split}

Also, let
\varphi_{R}(x,v)=(n_{x}\cdot v)\chi_{R}
, where
\chi_{R}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{R+1})
is the cutoff as introduced near (
2.19
). Observing that

\displaystyle\gamma(h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-h_{\varepsilon_{1}})^{2}=(\gamma(h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-h_{\varepsilon_{1}}))^{2}=(\gamma h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-\gamma h_{\varepsilon_{1}})^{2},

we can use the Green’s formula with
\varphi_{R}
as a test function in (
3.37
), to write an estimate for

(3.38)

\begin{split}&\iiint_{(0,T)\times\Omega\times B_{R}}|\gamma h_{\varepsilon_{2}}-\gamma h_{\varepsilon_{1}}|^{2}(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\,\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t,\end{split}

in almost the same way as we did in (
2.19
). Then, utilizing the convergences (
3.27
)–(
3.28
)–(
3.29
), noting that
\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{1},\mathscr{C}_{\varepsilon}^{2}\in L^{2}_{t,x}L^{2}_{v,{\rm loc}}
, and keeping in mind that
\sigma[h],\zeta[h],\eta[h],P[h]
are members of
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}
, we can use the obtained estimate to prove as in (
2.20
) that (
3.38
) vanishes as
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\to 0
. Therefore
(\gamma h_{\varepsilon})
is Cauchy in
L^{2}((0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times B_{R},(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t)
, and there is some limit
\gamma^{\prime}
for which up to a diagonal extraction
\gamma h_{\varepsilon}\to\gamma^{\prime}
a.e. and strongly in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma^{T},(n_{x}\cdot v)^{2}\textnormal{d}\sigma_{x}\textnormal{d}v\textnormal{d}t)
. Using this convergence, we can then pass to the limit in the Green’s formula satisfied by
(h_{\varepsilon},\gamma h_{\varepsilon})
in (
3.31
), to deduce by uniqueness of the trace that
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma h
.

Now coming back to the renormalized equation for
h_{\varepsilon}
, we write the Green’s formula for (
3.33
) satisfied by
(\beta(h_{\varepsilon}),\beta(\gamma h_{\varepsilon}))
. Since
\beta\in W^{2,\infty}
, the strong convergence
\gamma h_{\varepsilon}\to\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma h
implies
\beta(\gamma h_{\varepsilon})\to\beta(\gamma h)
a.e. Hence we can pass to the limit and find that
(\beta(h),\beta(\gamma h))
satisfies the Green’s formula for (
3.36
), with
\gamma\beta(h)=\beta(\gamma h)
. The proof is now complete.
∎

3.5.
Proof of Theorem
II

Writing
f=g+h
, thanks to the regularity assumption on
g
and recalling the embedding
L^{\infty}_{\omega}\hookrightarrow L^{2}
, we immediately obtain that
f\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}\cap L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x,v}\cap L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v}
and
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f_{t}-g_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})}\leq a
, along with the fact that
f
is a solution to (
1.1
) in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}([0,T]\times\overline{\mathcal{O}})
. In particular, using that
f\in L^{2}_{t,x}H^{1}_{v}
we may repeat Step 3, Proof of Proposition
3.9
(with no changes in the proof) to deduce that
f
is a renormalized solution to (
1.1
) satisfying the Green’s formula (
2.4
). Finally, if it is the case that
f_{0}
satisfies the conservation laws in (C), we may use the fact that
L^{\infty}_{\omega}(\mathcal{O})\hookrightarrow L^{1}_{\langle v\rangle^{2}}(\mathcal{O})
to justify the admission of
(1,R(x)\cdot v,|v|^{2})
as test functions in the Green’s formula for
f
. Thanks to the pointwise decay of
f
(remember that
f\in L^{\infty}_{\omega}
) any applications of the Gauss–Ostrogradsky theorem are easily justified and after some basic computations, we deduce that (
1.4
)–(
1.5
) hold for every
R\in\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}
, which imply that
f
satisfies (C).

4.
On the close-to-Maxwellian regime

4.1.
Setup of the problem

In this section, we prove Theorem
III
. We consider the close to equilibrium regime and set

f=\mu+h.

Thus
h
again corresponds to the perturbation as in Section
3.1
, but for the particular choice of
g=\mu
. By formally computing the Gateaux derivative of the nonlinear Fokker–Planck operator
\mathscr{F}
at
\mu
(see Appendix
A
), we find that the following linear operator acts as its first order approximation

\mathsf{C}_{\rm lin}h:=\Delta_{v}h+\nabla_{v}\cdot(vh)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\pi_{i}h+2\pi_{d+1}h,

where we use the following notations for the macroscopic projections in the space
L^{2}_{\mu^{-1/2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

\pi_{i}h:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(w)\textnormal{d}w\right)\mu(v)=\rho[h]\mu,&i=0,\\
\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}w_{i}h(w)\textnormal{d}w\right)v_{i}\,\mu(v)=j[h]_{i}v_{i}\mu,&i=1,2,\ldots,d,\\
\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|w|^{2}-d}{\sqrt{2d}}h(w)\,\textnormal{d}w\right)&\frac{|v|^{2}-d}{\sqrt{2d}}\mu(v)\\
&\hskip-85.35826pt=\frac{1}{2d}(E[h]-d\rho[h])(|v|^{2}-d)\mu,\end{aligned}&i=d+1.\end{cases}

Now writing the equation formally satisfied by
h:=f-\mu
, then subtracting the linear part
-v\cdot\nabla_{x}h+\mathscr{L}h
from the equation, we find that the nonlinear remainder is given by

(4.1)

\begin{split}\Upsilon(h)&:=\mathscr{F}[f]-\mathsf{C}_{\rm lin}h\\
&=\frac{1}{d}\left(E[h]-\frac{|j[h]|^{2}}{(1+\rho[h])}\right)\Delta_{v}h+(v\rho[h]-j[h])\cdot\nabla_{v}h\\
&\quad+d\rho[h]h-\frac{|j[h]|^{2}}{d(1+\rho[h])}(|v|^{2}-d)\mu.\end{split}

Towards the end of working in a setting which is compatible with the conservation laws of (
1.1
) (see (C)), we now make the following observations. First, we can easily check that the same conservation properties of
\mathscr{F}
hold true for the linearized operator
\mathsf{C}_{\rm lin}
. That is, for every
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
sufficiently integrable:

(4.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1,v,|v|^{2})\mathsf{C}_{\rm lin}h\,\textnormal{d}v=0.

It follows as a consequence of this and (
1.3
) that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1,v,|v|^{2})\Upsilon(h)\,\textnormal{d}v=0,

so that
\Upsilon(h)={}^{\perp}\!\!\!\>\Upsilon(h)
, where
{}^{\perp}\!\!\!\>\Upsilon(h):=(I-\pi)\Upsilon(h)
and
\pi:=\sum_{i=0}^{d+1}\pi_{i}
. In particular, we are tempted to write as in
[
11
]
:

\Upsilon(h)={}^{\perp}\!\!\!\>\Upsilon[h]h,

by defining for each given function
k=k(t,x,v)
, the linear operator
\Upsilon[k]
as

\Upsilon[k]h:=\frac{1}{d}\left(E[k]-\frac{|j[k]|^{2}}{1+\rho[k]}\right)\Delta_{v}h+(v\rho[k]-j[k])\cdot\nabla_{v}h+d\rho[k]h-\frac{1}{d}\frac{|j[k]|^{2}}{1+\rho[k]}(|v|^{2}-d)\mu.

However, as we previously observed in Section
3.1
, the quadratic diffusion coefficient is ill-behaved with respect to weak
-*
convergence. Therefore, we are motivated to use again the trick of employing convolution operations and define

\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[k]h:=\frac{1}{d}\left(E[k]-{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\right)\Delta_{v}h+(v\rho[k]-j[k])\cdot\nabla_{v}h+d\rho[k]h-\frac{1}{d}{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k](|v|^{2}-d)\mu,

where we used the same notation as in Section
3.1
:

{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]:=\frac{|j[k]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}|^{2}}{1+\rho[k]*_{t}{\bf s}_{\varsigma}\star_{\varsigma}{\bf r}_{\varsigma}},\quad\varsigma>0.

Then, observing that

\displaystyle\rho[\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[k]h]=0,

\displaystyle j[\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[k]h]=\rho[h]j[k]-\rho[k]j[h],

\displaystyle E[\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[k]h]=2\left(E[k]-{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\right)\rho[h]-2\rho[k]E[h]+2j[k]\cdot j[h]-2{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k],

we obtain

(4.3)

\begin{split}{}^{\perp}\!\!\!\>\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[k]h&:=(I-\pi)\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[k]h\\
&=\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[k]h-j[\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[k]h]\cdot v\mu-\frac{1}{2d}E[\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[k]f](|v|^{2}-d)\mu\\
&=\frac{1}{d}\left(E[k]-{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\right)\Delta_{v}h+(v\rho[k]-j[k])\cdot\nabla_{v}h+d\rho[k]h\\
&\quad-(\rho[h]j[k]-\rho[k]j[h])\cdot v\mu\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{d}\Big((E[k]-{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k])\rho[h]-\rho[k]E[h]+j[k]\cdot j[h]\Big)(|v|^{2}-d)\mu.\end{split}

At last, we denote

\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]:=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\mathsf{C}_{\rm lin}+{}^{\perp}\!\!\!\>\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[k],

whose explicit form is given by

\begin{split}&\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]h=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}h+\left(1+\frac{1}{d}E[k]-\frac{1}{d}{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\right)\Delta_{v}h\\
&\qquad\qquad+\big(v(1+\rho[k])-j[k]\big)\cdot\nabla_{v}h+d(1+\rho[k])h\\
&\qquad\qquad+\Big((1+\rho[k])j[h]-j[k]\rho[h]\Big)\cdot v\mu\\
&\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{d}\left(-\Big(d+E[k]-{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\Big)\rho[h]-j[k]\cdot j[h]+(1+\rho[k])E[h]\right)(|v|^{2}-d)\mu,\end{split}

and our goal is to investigate the properties of the now linear evolution equation

(4.4)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}h=\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]h,\\
h|_{t=0}=h_{0},\end{cases}

equipped with the specular reflection boundary condition (
1.2
). Once all of the desired properties have been deduced from (
4.4
), we will commence a fixed point argument to transition all of those properties to the nonlinear problem

(4.5)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}h=\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[h]h=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}h+\mathsf{C}_{\rm lin}h+{}^{\perp}\!\!\!\>\Upsilon_{\varsigma}[h]h,\\
h|_{t=0}=h_{0},\\
\gamma_{-}h(t,x,v)=\gamma_{+}h(t,x,\mathscr{V}_{x}),\end{cases}

see Proposition
4.22
. Finally, in Proposition
4.23
, we pass to the limit
\varsigma\to 0
to obtain a weak solution to

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}h=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}h+\mathsf{C}_{\rm lin}h+\Upsilon(h),\\
h|_{t=0}=h_{0},\\
\gamma_{-}h(t,x,v)=\gamma_{+}h(t,x,\mathscr{V}_{x}),\end{cases}

We close this section by mentioning that from hereon, we keep a weight
\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
fixed. Furthermore, we will impose the assumption that the given function
k=k(t,x,v)
is extracted from
\mathcal{X}_{\omega,c}
, which is defined for each
c>0
as

\mathcal{X}_{\omega,c}:=\left\{k:(0,\infty)\times\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}\;\middle|\;\|k\|_{L^{\infty}_{\omega}((0,\infty)\times\mathcal{O})}\leq c\right\}.

In order to simplify the notation, we will also adopt the nomenclature

\displaystyle\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]:=\left(1+\frac{1}{d}E[k]-\frac{1}{d}{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\right),

\displaystyle\zeta[k]:=v(1+\rho[k])-j[k],\quad\eta[k]:=d(1+\rho[k]).

In this way, we can write the collisional part of the operator
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
as

\mathsf{L}_{\varsigma}[k]h:=\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\Delta_{v}h+\zeta[k]\cdot\nabla_{v}h+\eta[k]h.

Then, setting
\mathsf{P}_{\varsigma}[k]
as the projection part of
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
:

(4.6)

\begin{split}\mathsf{P}_{\varsigma}[k]h&:=\Big((1+\rho[k])j[h]-j[k]\rho[h]\Big)\cdot v\mu\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{d}\left(-\Big(d+E[k]-{\bf A}_{\varsigma}[k]\Big)\rho[h]-j[k]\cdot j[h]+(1+\rho[k])E[h]\right)(|v|^{2}-d)\mu,\end{split}

we have that

\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\mathsf{L}_{\varsigma}[k]+\mathsf{P}_{\varsigma}[k].

4.2.
Sketch of the proof of Theorem
III

Motivated by the preceding works
[
10
,
23
,
39
]
, we also factorize
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
in a different direction, by writing

\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]=\mathsf{G}_{\varsigma}[k]+\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k],

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{\varsigma}[k]

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{P}_{\varsigma}[k]+M\psi_{R}(v),

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]

\displaystyle:=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\mathsf{L}_{\varsigma}[k]-M\psi_{R}.

Here,
\psi_{R}=\psi_{R}(v)
is a smooth radial cutoff function satisfying

\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq R}\leq\psi_{R}\leq\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq 2R},

and
M,R>1
are large constants to be determined later. Below is a sketch of proof of Theorem
III
. As aforementioned, most of the work is done on the linear problem associated to
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
, and we pass the results to the original model later.

The ingredients of Section
4.3
:

(1)

In Lemma
4.1
, we prove that the evolution equation corresponding to
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
is well-posed in
L^{p}_{\omega_{1}}
whenever
\omega_{1}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
and
p\in[2,\infty]
, by manipulating the class of polynomial weights in
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
(we use the same notation as in Section
3.1
here).

(2)

In Proposition
4.4
, we prove that whenever the factorization
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]=\mathsf{G}_{\varsigma}[k]+\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
is done suitably (that is, for all appropriately large choices of
M,R
), the flow corresponding to
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
is dissipative in
L^{p}_{\omega_{1}}
. This is done by working on the backwards equation, see Lemma
4.3
, and resorting to duality results.

The ingredients of Section
4.4
: the goal of this section is summarized in Proposition
4.10
. Informally, this proposition states that for any suitable exponential weight
{\bf w}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
, the flow corresponding to
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
satisfies an
L^{2}_{{\bf w}}\to L^{\infty}_{\omega_{\star}}
ultracontractivity estimate, for every suitable choice of weight
\omega_{\star}\in\mathcal{W}
. The proof of Proposition
4.10
goes as follows:

(1)

As in Section
4.3
, we work on the backwards dual equation corresponding to
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]^{*}
, and therefore we use a dual weight
\mathfrak{m}
such that
\mathfrak{m}^{-1}={\bf w}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
. By incorporating an appropriate multiplier in the spirit of
[
10
,
38
,
41
]
, we adapt the estimates of
[
10
]
to prove a boundary penalizing estimate for the backwards dual equation in Lemma
4.5
.

(2)

Next, in Lemma
4.7
, we prove a local ultracontractivity estimate for the backwards equation by taking advantage of the Green’s function known in the whole space.

(3)

Combining these two results, we end up with an
L^{2}\to L^{r}
estimate (with
r>2
) in Lemma
4.8
that holds
up to the boundary
.

(4)

At last, by using some classical estimates in the spirit of Nash, we obtain the
L^{1}\to L^{2}
estimate for the backwards equation in Lemma
4.9
. By duality we recover the
L^{2}\to L^{\infty}
estimate promised in Proposition
4.10
.

The ingredients of Sections
4.5
–
4.6
: From hereon, we end our discussion of
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
, and aim to establish properties of the full operator
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
.

(1)

In Proposition
4.11
, we prove that the evolution equation corresponding to
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
is well-posed in the reference space
L^{2}_{\omega_{1}}
, whenever
\omega_{1}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
and
k
is chosen suitably small corresponding to
\omega_{1}
.

(2)

In Proposition
4.12
we prove that the flow corresponding to
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
dissipates exponentially to the global Maxwellian, in the reference space
L^{2}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
. This is done by adapting the
L^{2}
-hypocoercivity framework of
[
4
]
, and by revisiting computations on the
linear
Fokker–Planck operator, making sure that the estimates are refined enough so that all unwanted terms arising from the nonlinearity can be absorbed.

The ingredients of Section
4.7
:

(1)

Using an extension trick, we prove in the spirit of
[
11
]
that the evolution system corresponding to
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
is hypodissipative in
L^{\infty}_{\omega}
. This result is summarized in Proposition
4.18
.

(2)

The proof of Proposition
4.18
is based on an iterated Duhamel formula as in
[
23
,
39
]
which is reminiscent of the Dyson series. The results of Proposition
4.18
make crucial use of all of the previous results in Sections
4.3
–
4.4
–
4.5
–
4.6
. We take care in ensuring that all of the estimates can be made independent of the regularization parameter
\varsigma
.

The ingredients of Sections
4.8
–
4.9
:

(1)

In Proposition
4.22
, we perform a fixed point argument
k\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]}(t,0)h_{0}
, where
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]}
is the evolution system corresponding to
\mathsf{F}_{\varsigma}[k]
, in order to prove the existence of a solution to the nonlinear (but regularized) equation (
4.5
).

(2)

At last, in Proposition
4.23
, we pass to the limit
\varsigma\to 0
to prove the existence of a weak solution
h
to the full equation
\partial_{t}h+v\cdot\nabla_{x}h=\mathsf{C}_{\rm lin}h+\Upsilon(h)
, which satisfies the decay as claimed in Theorem
III
.

(3)

The proof of Theorem
III
then follows as a consequence of all we have established so far, and we sketch it in Section
4.9
.

4.3.
Well-posedness and dissipativity of
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]

In this section, we investigate the well-posedness of

(4.7)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}h=\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]h,\\
\gamma_{-}h=\gamma_{+}h\circ\mathscr{V}_{x},\\
h|_{t=0}=h_{0},\end{cases}

and establish its dissipative properties for suitably large
M,R
.

Lemma 4.1
.

There exists
c_{1}>0
small enough (independent of
\varsigma
) such that for any
c\leq c_{1}
,
k\in\mathcal{X}_{\omega,c}
, and
M,R\geq 1
, the following holds true: for any
p\in[2,\infty]
,
\omega_{1}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
, and
h_{0}\in L^{p}_{\omega_{1}}(\mathcal{O})
, the linear problem (
4.7
) associated to
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
admits a unique solution
h
which lies in
C([0,T];L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O}))
for all weights
\widetilde{\omega}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\widetilde{\omega}\omega_{1}^{-1}\in L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof
. The proof follows the method of Lemma
3.1
. We fix
\widetilde{\omega}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
growing slowly enough so that

\widetilde{\omega}\omega_{1}^{-1}\in L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

In particular
\widetilde{\omega}\omega_{1}^{-1}\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
q\in[2,\infty]
, and this implies that the embedding
L^{p}_{\omega_{1}}\hookrightarrow L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}
is continuous. Then provided that
c_{1}
is chosen small enough so that
c\leq c_{1}
and
k\in\mathcal{X}_{\omega,c}
imply at least

(4.8)

(|\rho[k]|+|j[k]|+|E[k]|)\leq\frac{1}{10},\quad\frac{9}{10}\leq\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\leq\frac{11}{10},

we may apply Proposition
2.1
, to deduce that for given
h_{0}\in L^{p}_{\omega_{1}}(\mathcal{O})
there is a unique corresponding solution
h\in C([0,T];L^{2}_{\widetilde{\omega}}(\mathcal{O}))
(notice that (L2) is automatically satisfied because the weight
\widetilde{\omega}
is a polynomial; see for instance Lemma
4.2
below).
∎

Note that the above lemma only provides the well-posedness of (
4.7
) in a weighted
L^{2}
-framework; it does not tell us whether the solution to (
4.7
) actually lies in
C([0,T];L^{p}_{\omega_{1}}(\mathcal{O}))
. For this reason, we now commence an argument reminiscent of those in Lemmas
3.2
–
3.3
and Proposition
3.6
. Namely, we consider the backwards dual problem

(4.9)

\begin{cases}-\partial_{t}b=\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]^{*}b,\\
\gamma_{+}h=\gamma_{-}h\circ\mathscr{V}_{x},\\
b|_{t=T}=b_{T},\end{cases}

where
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]^{*}
is the formal adjoint of
\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k]^{*}b

\displaystyle:=v\cdot\nabla_{x}b+\sigma_{\varsigma}[k]\Delta_{v}b-\nabla_{v}\cdot(\zeta[k]b)+\eta[k]b-M\psi_{R}b.

Prior to stating the lemma which provides the dissipative structure of (
4.9
), let us briefly revisit the computations near (
3.5
) and establish the following asymptotic behavior of
\Gamma_{\mathsf{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega_{1},p}
for
\omega_{1}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
, in order to avoid repeating many arguments.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\omega_{1}=\langle v\rangle^{m}e^{\gamma|v|^{\tau}}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
. Then

(1)

If
\gamma=0
so that
\omega_{1}=\langle v\rangle^{m}
, then
c\leq c_{1}
and
k\in\mathcal{X}_{\omega,c}
implies

\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega}\sup_{p\in[1,\infty]}\limsup_{|v|\to\infty}\Gamma_{\mathsf{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega_{1},p}\leq-1.

(2)

If
\tau\in(0,2)
,
m\geq 0
, and
\gamma>0
, then
c\leq c_{1}
and
k\in\mathcal{X}_{\omega,c}
implies

\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega}\sup_{p\in[1,\infty]}\limsup_{|v|\to\infty}\Gamma_{\mathsf{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega_{1},p}=-\infty.

(3)

If
\tau=2
,
m\geq 0
, and
\gamma\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
, then there exists
c_{\omega_{1}}>0
(independent of
\varsigma
) such that whenever
c\leq\min\{c_{1},c_{\omega_{1}}\}
and
k\in\mathcal{X}_{\omega,c}
,

\sup_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega}\sup_{p\in[1,\infty]}\limsup_{|v|\to\infty}\Gamma_{\mathsf{L}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega_{1},p}=-\infty.

For the sake of convenience, we define the convention
c_{\omega_{1}}=+\infty
if either
\gamma=0
or
\tau\in(0,2)
.

Proof
. We merely recall the formulae in (
3.5
) and compute directly.

(1) If
\gamma=0
, then by definition of
\mathcal{W}_{\mu^{-1/2}}
we have
m>d+2
, and thus for all
p\in[1,\infty]
:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega_{1},p}

\displaystyle\sim-\zeta[k]\cdot\nabla_{v}W_{1}-\frac{1}{p}\nabla_{v}\cdot\zeta[k]+\eta[k]

\displaystyle\sim-m(1+\rho[k])+\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}\right)d(1+\rho[k])

\displaystyle\leq-(m-d)(1+\rho[k])

\displaystyle\leq-1\quad\text{as }|v|\to\infty,

because
|\rho[k]|\leq 1/2
.

(2) If
\gamma>0
and
\tau\in(0,2)
, then
-\zeta[k]\cdot\nabla_{v}W_{1}
is the only dominant term, and we can easily check that
\Gamma_{\mathsf{H}_{\varsigma}[k],\omega_{1},p}\to-\infty
unconditionally.

(3) If
\tau=2
and
\gamma\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
, we argue as in the proof of Lemma
3.3
. We have that
